#7100. This Colorful South Carolina Home Marries Mediterranean Elegance With Lowcountry Charm

Designer Megan Molten worked handmade details and custom elements into every room.

How do you blend the magical ease of Mediterranean life with the coastal charm of South Carolina’s lowcountry? After an enchanting vacation in Greece with their three daughters, Megan Molten’s clients wanted their new build on Daniel Island to capture that unique, transportive feeling they fell in love with—and they asked the Charleston-based designer to help them achieve it.

Alongside builder Wes Whittle of EasTech Property Development, Molten was involved from the very beginning, crafting the blank canvas into a residence that balanced the sun-drenched elegance of the Greek islands with traditional Lowcountry living. The designer went all in on every detail, relying on high-quality craftsmanship, custom pieces, and handmade elements to bring life to every room.

Upon entry, guests are greeted by a hand-painted, botanical-themed mural by local artist Jenn Griffith. “The mural’s objective was very specific,” says Molten. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry.” Turn to the other side and find a plaster-covered stairwell by artist Emily Pope Harris, an addition that sets the tone for the whole home. Move further into the home and find a hand-painted floor that shines in the craft room, created using a stencil from Etsy and a few Sherwin-Williams paint colors—it’s playfully fabulous and still somehow timeless. “Clients do not typically splurge in rooms that are not front and center, so this was a real treat to bring to life, especially knowing how much their daughters love to craft,” the designer adds.

Elsewhere, a larger portion of the budget was split between the lighting and the kitchen. “Statement lighting in the house was always prioritized,” Molten says. “The homeowners also agreed that the lighting was important and needed to be special. I was finally able to use some light fixtures that I have been eyeing for years!”

As for the kitchen, it was more difficult to know where to splurge and where to save, a predicament the designer finds herself with on most projects. However, looking now at the honed limestone backsplash, green quartzite countertops with an integrated sink, and a plaster hood by Pope Harris, it’s clear that she made the right choices.

Even with all the home’s beautiful elements, the fan-favorite among Molten’s team ended up being the pool just outside the kitchen. It’s lower than most Molten has worked on in Charleston, making it one of the most unique she’s seen. Flanked by archways and paired with two spas and a raised hot tub, it fully captures a Grecian grotto feel.

Even with all the one-of-a-kind features and elevated furnishings, the home is a place where life is meant to be experienced to the fullest. “The spaces are to be enjoyed without hesitation,” Molten explains, “creating a beautiful, practical environment for a life well lived.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Megan MoltenArchitect: DLB Custom Home DesignBuilder: EasTech Property DevelopmentLocation: Daniel Island, South CarolinaThe Space: A six-bedroom, seven-bathroom new build spanning 6,000 square feet.

The beauty of this entryway starts outside with the front door. Made locally by Maebeck Doors, it’s an arched shape with a grid pattern that serves as a charming “wow” moment upon entering the home.

Once inside the house, the awe continues, from the curved plaster stairwell to the custom botanical mural by artist Jenn Griffith. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry,” Molten adds. The final touch? A stone table in the notch at the base of the stairs, perfect for placing keys or bags at the end of the day.

Molten’s client wanted a large, comfortable sectional that could easily seat her family and any guests, so when the team saw these two at High Point Market, they knew they were perfect for the space. A travertine table, nestled between the two sectionals, made the pieces feel like one long, continuous design, and both were upholstered in Crypton performance fabric for added durability.

To complement the space, Molten added a plush armchair and topped the room off with a modern light fixture that doesn’t add much noise. “The artwork above the sectional is from the homeowner’s own collection,” Molten says. “It served as the catalyst for the colorways used throughout the home.”

A calming, functional space, the brightness of the kitchen is achieved not through a classic all-white kitchen, but through shades of light green, from the quartzite countertops to the greenish-gray cabinets. Rattan pendant lights bring in that casual Mediterranean feel, especially since the floor-to-ceiling windows on the left overlook the pool below.

To keep it family-friendly, Molten applied one of her favorite tricks: leather on the counter stool seats and fabric on the back. “Wiping off messes and letting the leather patina is both practical and pretty,” she adds.

This dining nook really needed to be functional, since it’s where Molten’s clients and their three children sit for breakfast every morning and dinner every night. Durability for daily use played a huge part in the furniture choices; they sourced a long wooden table and used the same seating upholstery trick here as they did with the counter stools in the kitchen. For a touch of whimsy, Molten crowned the nook with a coco bead chandelier.

Instead of plaster on the double-sided fireplace as she had initially intended, Molten went with a zellige tile façade that produces a lovely shine.

Easily accessible from the living room, kitchen, and screened-in porch, this breakfast area—complete with a full bar—is perfect for hosting. Anyone can come in and make themselves a drink whenever they want.

Arched upper cabinets with lattice details create more movement in this space, as well as add in charm, and the chandelier has been on the designer’s list of favorites for a while. “I particularly love the contrast of the black countertop with the coral cabinets,” Molten says.

The first thing Molten selected for the scullery was the charming lattice window over the sink looking out onto the yard. Then came the bold wallpaper, which inspired the vibrant cabinet paint color, balanced out with a light Calacatta marble countertop with hints of yellow and gold throughout.

“This scullery is a very functional space that houses their microwave, coffee maker, refrigerator, and drawers with snacks, all built-in and panel-ready,” Molten says.

When her clients told her they wanted a dedicated craft room for their girls, Molten was just as excited at the prospect as their daughters. “A dedicated specialty room like this typically provides a great opportunity for lots of creativity and color,” she explains.

They started with the colorful striped wallpaper on the ceiling, pulling colors from it for the built-in cubby shelf and the painted floors. A neutral light fixture keeps the room from feeling too busy, giving it a more timeless feel. “The floor design is so special, and a bit of a splurge,” Molten adds. “I was so pleased the client embraced this idea!”

The unique wallpaper served as the starting point for this powder bath, as Molten designed everything else around it. “The Jamie Young mirror looks like an oyster shell, and the floating stone was a nice feature,” she says. “Painting the trim and ceiling the pink shell color made the wallpaper, tile, and stone come together.”

Molten had never done a wallpaper border before, but she adores how it turned out in one of the daughter’s bedrooms. “It’s a great way to add thoughtful design details to a room and is cost-effective too, as opposed to covering the full room in wallpaper,” she says. The trim color was pulled from this border, which she paired with a signature shade of pink from her own collection with Sherwin-Williams.

“For the daughter’s bathroom, we opted for a tub/shower combo to accommodate young children,” Molten explains. These children-friendly details are found elsewhere in the room too, like a pull-out step stool under the sink cabinets.

The pink-and-green palette from the daughter’s bedroom is continued here, though in slightly more saturated shades. A local Charleston brand, Mirth Studio, created the painted wood valance to pair with the Pepper Home shower curtain, and Pope Harris continued her plaster work here, covering a Visual Comfort & Co. sconce over the mirror in a custom pink hue.

Located on the small third floor, the only rooms that occupy this level are the office, the office den, and a guest suite, making it a quiet place to escape when the clients, who share this space, need to get work done. “Our client loved the Tiger Palm wallpaper, so we applied that on all of the walls and at the low part of the angled ceiling to help mask all of the ceiling lines in this room,” Molten says. They even have their own wet bar up here for post-work happy hour.

Designed as a dedicated no-kids zone off the office, this private room is where Molten’s clients get away to watch movies after bedtime, decompress after a long day, and just retreat to when they need time to themselves.

“We opted for a performance velvet on the sofa with velvet textured pillows to create a luxurious, cozy feel,” the designer says. “I love the Currey & Co. chandelier with green glass—it works so perfectly in this small room.”

“Like the primary bedroom and primary bathroom, the closet is not huge, but it’s efficient and thoughtfully designed,” Molten explains. With her team, they designed a wall of built-ins and paid close attention to the functionality of each drawer and shelf while maximizing the space; then a local cabinet maker brought them to life.

Molten chose soft, luxurious carpet that feels like stepping into a high-end department store and a fabulous Made Goods light fixture.

The architect who worked on this house, Damien Busillo of DLB Custom Home Design, had this lower-level pool incorporated in the original plans, and Molten was able to assist with the tile selections.

“This is such a creative way to use empty space under the house,” she says. “Homes in Charleston are often raised when built due to coastal flooding; however, this was the most interesting and exciting design concept I have seen for this area of the home.” It truly captures that Grecian grotto vibe.

Megan Molten has always been creative, but her love of interiors didn’t fully manifest until she bought and decorated her first house in Charleston, South Carolina. From there, she started experimenting with design as a hobby, sharing her eye with friends and family, before devoting all her time to it and finally deciding to open her own eponymous firm. Now, she shares her elevated coastal style and luxurious Lowcountry vibe with clients across the country.

#7098. I'm the Friend Everyone Texts for Tableware Recs. Here's Where I Send Them

Consider this your shortcut to a beautifully dressed table.

The art of tablescaping starts with some really beautiful tableware. No disrespect to placemats, but the glasses, plates, and cutlery that your guests use tend to take the spotlight. Everything tastes better coming out of a fun wine glass. And presentation is key. The right serving platter makes your weeknight leftovers look Michelin-starred. We know your design taste is as unique as your palate, so we wanted to make your shopping experience as seamless as possible. Below, we reveal over a dozen of the very best tableware brands on the market.

Whether you’re a frequent dinner party host or you’re romanticizing dinners for one, you deserve to find something truly unique for your dining table. We focused our search on dinnerware, glassware, cutlery, and serving essentials. (Table linens matter, but that’s a whole other article.) Of course, we started with the pieces filling our editors’ kitchens and dining rooms. That’s where we keep the cake plates for our favorite little treats, and high-quality wine glasses that aren’t too fragile for busy kitchens. But trust us, after going to the best design markets in the country and abroad, these are the brands that wow us year after year. They’re built to last and make every host’s life way easier.

East Fork was founded by Henri Matisse’s great-grandson, and the artistry shows in every piece. The hand-thrown pottery is crafted from regionally sourced clay in Asheville, North Carolina. The lightly speckled, colorful stoneware plates are a favorite of Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie, especially the Matisse Cake Plate sets.

Fable is a tableware brand designed for the frequent family dinner host. The brand’s Canyon Clay collection brings a warm, sunbaked terracotta to their durable serveware. It’s dishwasher-safe and meant to hold up to your messiest meals, with a touch of elegance.

If you’re looking for the kind of beautiful plates and cups that can hold up to outdoor use, look no further than Thomas Fuchs’s melamine collection. These chip-proof, shatter-proof plates are designed for al fresco meals, and the half-and-half design adds some playful color.

We already loved Hommey for their viral striped cotton bedding, but the brand has brought that signature look to your tabletop. Even your cutlery deserves a little color-drenching, and certain colors are already selling out. It’s the kind of small upgrade that makes every meal feel a bit more special.

Let’s talk glassware: no one does clarity like Simon Pearce. It catches the light beautifully. Every single glassware piece from this brand is handmade by one of a few expert artisans, and the imperfection of handmade goods is celebrated here. (You can even get the extra quirky ones—called Seconds—for a discounted price.)

Chances are you’ve seen Serax tableware in a designer department store. The brand has decades of tabletop excellence under its belt, and its designer collaborations result in delightfully creative pieces. The Carte Blanche collection, designed to look like hand-drawn sketches, is one of our favorites.

Anthropologie is the home of little luxuries for everywhere in your home, but we’re especially partial to their small space-friendly tabletop pieces. Espresso cups and dessert plates just make breakfast feel way more glamorous. The gilded Bistro Tile collection is perfect for anyone wanting some Parisian flair in their kitchen.

Sabre is another iconic name in the world of tabletop, and one look at their stainless steel cutlery sets will tell you why. You can choose handles crafted from teak wood, tortoiseshell or a glossy lacquer, and you’ll love setting the table with them at dinnertime.

It really is the little things that make a difference, and at Mogutable, those are the irresistibly cute chopsticks rests. Imagine a few of these adorable food-shaped rests scattered across your dinner table, or letting each dinner guest select one of their own before noodles are served.

Arhaus is already one of our favorite furniture brands, so it’s no surprise the sophisticated home destination has incredible serveware. Their dinnerware sets get pretty pricey, so we suggest starting with their hosting essentials: napkin rings, coasters, and cocktail shakers that help you host like a pro.

Would you believe it if we told you those are paper plates? Table Two Studio is all about disposable plates for easy cleanup, without sacrificing style. The botanical patterns remind us of archival fabric prints from our favorite textile houses. Your guests will feel extra special, and you won’t spend hours doing dishes.

The Museum of Modern Art really knows how to curate an inspiring collection, and that extends to their home goods. It’s McKenzie’s first stop whenever she wants to find a cool hostess gift. Their glassware is unmatched, especially for any fans of colored glass or playful motifs.

Fishs Eddy dinnerware pays homage to a symbol of Americana and hospitality: the diner. Their green-band dishes, checkered plates, and simple white mugs are all evocative of a steaming diner breakfast after a long night. All of that cozy nostalgia warms up your family meals.

If you love all things spiraled and swirly, this is the glassware brand for you. The delicate pops of color and squiggled stems on the glass pitchers make even water look like a fun beverage. Perfect for cocktail hour, and sure to be treasured.

If you want to spot an Ichendorf Milano glass in the wild, look for colorful motifs from nature. A stem shaped like Lily of the Valley flowers or a fish at the bottom of a tumbler are telltale signs. It’s the kind of glassware you’d love to put on your registry and display.

Jono Pandolfi plates are a fine dining staple. They’re served at luxury restaurants across the country—you can even see them listed on the page of each glaze color. So if you like to get experimental in the kitchen and want your dishes to look Michelin star, you can literally use what the pros use.

The brand that ushered in a ceramic cookware takeover also has beautiful ceramic tableware. They have dinnerware bundles that are easy to build on over time, perfect for cozy brunches even in tiny apartment kitchens to feel a little fancier. It’s a starter set that doesn’t feel like it’s from the bargain bin.

#7093. 36 Thoughtful Kitchen Gifts for Anyone Who Loves to Cook, Host, or Sip in Style

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Anyone can buy a candle. These gifts are way more personal.

The best kind of gift shows the person you care about that you know them. That you’ve considered their hobbies. After all, anyone can go out and buy a scented candle, but you are thinking about how they like to spend their free time and what makes them happy. So if you know a dinner party host, mixologist, home chef, or someone who loves a stylish kitchen, then this is the gift guide for you.

Below, dozens of smart and thoughtful kitchen gifts await you. The busy home cook will love an Instant Pot or multipurpose air fryer. The coffee or tea obsessive will love a matcha station or coffee grinder. Think outside the kitchen and into the dining room, too: an elegant yet budget-friendly canister set or berry basket could be just what they need to complete their tablescape.

The best thing about so many of our picks is that there’s a massive price range, so if you see something affordable your friend or family member may like, you don’t need to wait for a special occasion to get it for them. On the other hand, if the same person is celebrating a birthday or a holiday, feel free to splurge. We have everything any at-home cook would love and use.

No kitchen is complete without a good bottle opener, so why not opt for one that’s extra chic? Trade your gas station souvenir opener for this colorful beaded piece, inspired by the classic abacus tool. You can even rearrange the beads to suit your own aesthetic.

Any serious coffee drinker or amateur barista will know the importance of a good coffee bean grinder. There’s nothing quite like being able to control the coarseness of your own coffee for your morning brew. You can choose between 60 grind sizes, whether you’re using a moka pot or prepping cold brew.

If your date night dinner isn’t complete without a round of oysters, then this oyster plate needs to be added to your collection. The ridged porcelain edge looks like a more elegant version of a paper plate. And the indents in the dish are perfect for your East Coast dozen. It’s even dishwasher-safe.

This is the ideal martini glass for your most stylish friend. The color-blocked stems are thin enough for a swirl, and the set of two glasses comes in alternating colors. It even comes with stainless steel swizzle sticks, primed for stuffed olives.

Gift a set of serving utensils that’s a major upgrade from the typical silicone and plastic. The lustrous finish and curved handles are just too beautiful. It’s carved from sustainably sourced horn and natural shell, and it’ll make your salad bowl look like the star of the show.

Rocco’s full-size fridges have gone viral for every sophisticated host. But the latest launch is way more budget-friendly. This mini mini fridge is sized for desktops and nightstands, so whether you need a nighttime face mask or a midday Diet Coke break, it’s stored in style.

Everyone knows at least one matcha drinker who is serious about their habit. This is the station they’ll reach for every morning. The bowl, bamboo whisk, whisk stand, and sifter all ensure a clump-free, perfectly aerated brew. And it’s all gathered on a ceramic, non-slip tea tray.

Know a tinned fish lover? Fishwife has made sardines sexy again, and this advent calendar provides 12 days of fun surprises for every charcuterie board assembler. It even comes with a few non-edible pieces like a fish knife, key chain, and moisturizer.

Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie has been using the electric kettle from Drew Barrymore’s line for years. It comes in a variety of pastel colors that brighten up your countertop and make short work of everything from loose-leaf tea to soup.

Any home chef serious about their spices needs a mortar and pestle that can keep up. It’s made from pure bronze, with an ergonomic handle that makes grinding peppercorns, cardamom, ginger, and garlic easy. It’s handmade by artisans in Moradabad, known as “The Brass City of India.”

These glasses look like they should be kept safe in a china cabinet, but they’re durable enough for everyday life. The shape of the glass was designed in collaboration with WhistlePig distillers to enhance the whiskey-drinking experience. The pontil mark on the bottom of each glass celebrates “perfect imperfection”—a mark leftover by Simon Pearce glassblowers.

A new paper towel holder is an upgrade you won’t think to do on your own, which makes it the perfect kitchen gift. It’s likely one of the most-visited spots in your kitchen. The painted beech wood and rubber feet keep it both stylish and secure.

Things get messy in the kitchen, and no one will say no to a gift of fresh dish towels. These are woven from organic cotton and stitched by a woman-owned textile company in Mumbai. They’re embroidered with the icons of Indian cooking: a fresh coconut, a tomato, and curry leaves.

Ash wood and granite stone are a match made in heaven to get the most of your ground pepper. McKenzie uses this with gourmet peppercorns, and with the easily refilled vessel and cut stone, it’s a tactile experience that elevates the simplest meal. The stone smooths over time as oils are released by the peppercorns, effectively seasoning it over time.

This is perfect for the pitmaster in your life. Sure, they've probably got a meat thermometer from the grocery store, but this upgrade is so worth it, especially for those long, slow-cooked meats. This high-tech, long-range Bluetooth thermometer will save them the headache of constantly checking their grill, so they can check its status from the comfort of the couch.

This is the potluck host’s secret weapon. No need to mess with the hassle of foil pans, chafing dishes, and Sterno burners. Just one glass-top electric warming tray can keep your appetizers hot for the whole party. It even comes with cool-touch handles for easy portability.

This is the hand wash McKenzie leaves out on her kitchen sink when guests come over. “The Moroccan mint scent is refreshing, and the lather is moisturizing, not stripping,” McKenzie says. “When the dinner party gets chaotic, this is the easiest way for guests to refresh themselves in the kitchen. The glass bottle is an extra sleek touch.”

There's a reason Drew Barrymore's slow cooker sold out within days of launching. Now, more than a year later, the hype has gone down a bit, making snatching one up a lot easier. It comes in a few delicious colors, but we're partial to the white one, which Barrymore aptly named White Icing.

Keep your favorite spices and garnishes within reach with this beautiful seasoning dish. It's about the capacity of a pinch bowl, but the two walnut wood compartments can hold salt, dried herbs, and even peppercorns and capers separately. Make seasoning food incredibly easy—with one pinch, any meal is elevated.

#7090. 13 Clever Details I Saw at the 2026 Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas

Check out the first ever home gym in Kips Bay Show House history.

The seventh annual Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas returns to 2999 Turtle Creek Boulevard, but don’t expect it to look anything like it did in 2025. A new group of the country’s leading designers has taken on the 25,000-square-foot French Renaissance estate, transforming each room to showcase bold ideas, highlight exceptional craftsmanship, and reveal the upcoming trends we can expect to see in the world of interior design.

Visit the show house and see each of the 22 designers’ rooms, from the first dedicated home gym in Kips Bay history by Dallas-based Platinum Gym Design, to a small but mighty European-inspired kitchen by Stephenson Home, to a 1970s-inspired primary bathroom fit with a glass brick shower and curtained-off water closet by Nuance Interiors. With sponsors like Benjamin Moore, The Shade Store, The Rug Company, and more, the vibrant and personality-filled spaces come as no surprise.

As always, proceeds from the show house go to the Kips Bay Boys & Girls Club, which provides educational, enrichment, and developmental programs to thousands of young people, as well as Dallas-based nonprofits Dwell with Dignity and The Crystal Charity Ball.

To see this celebration of exceptional design, you can purchase tickets here and visit the house from Saturday, November 14th to Sunday, December 6th. However, if you can’t make it to the Lone Star State in time, check out some of our favorite moments from the event below.

Kelsi Lamberth of Nuance Interiors pulled inspiration from the 1970s for her primary bathroom, separating the shower from the main prep space using a curved glass brick wall. The sculptural aspects can be found throughout in this groovy space.

In the primary bedroom, Troy Rivington of Rivington Marx Interiors cocooned the room in a dark floral wallpaper and accessorized with other moody botanicals throughout. He was inspired by the romance of 1940s Cairo, dressing the room up like it belonged to a well-traveled collector.

As the first ever dedicated home gym at the Kips Bay Show House, Peter Hoffman of Platinum Gym Design chose a refined, luxurious setup. He named the room Tanren after the Japanese art of hammering and refining metal, and used walnut in both the equipment and the ceiling slats, stainless steel frames, and brown leather accents to complete the exercise space.

In this bespoke bathroom, Russ Peters and Bill Cates of Peters Cates Design lined the wall behind the Studio McGee x Kohler bathtub with a rich merlot tile by RIAD Tile. It’s a beautiful statement and a fabulous way to bring more texture and dimension to the room.

In this tucked away library space, filled with collected finds, designers Christina Garcia Lysaught and Grayson Knight of Layered Dimensions finished off this dark space with a stunning statement mirror. Around the perimeter are resin busts of wildlife, like owls and deer, bringing in a bejeweled touch to the room.

The small but mighty kitchen, designed by Katie Stephenson of Stephenson Home, is crowned with a crackled-gold blooming wallpaper, bringing a warm balance to the rest of the room. It’s covered in birds, butterflies, and blossoms, and it wraps the cook space in a golden glow, reminding all who enter that this room should be as beautiful as it is functional.

The citron paint color wrapping the walls in Jen Bienvenu’s entrance hall is undoubtedly enchanting and serves as the perfect backdrop for her “After the Hunt” theme, but it’s the lampshades made from old silk saris that tie everything together for us. They provide such a magical warmth and really capture the essence of a collector’s treasure room.

Related Story: Almost Every Piece in This Layered Arkansas Home Was a Lucky Antique Find

Step through the citron entry and enter the round room, designed in collaboration by Helen Bergin Interiors and Lichten Architects. The walls are softened by long curtains from The Shade Store, which create beautiful juxtaposition with the curved, sculptural silver media console by Asher Gillman. The actual turntable is flush to the surface, giving it a seamless look while it emits wonderful sound.

In the Withdrawing Room by Jen Stevens of Fonde Interiors, plaster botanicals by artist Amanda Norman come to life as they crawl from the mantel along the chimney breast, escaping to the ceiling. The vines were handmade by Norman and capture the essence of a garden left undisturbed, showing that this formal-at-first-glance room has a wilder side.

The rosy palette in designer Jen Dean’s—founder of Jede Interiors—grand bathroom is topped off with a hand-painted floral mural that covers the ceiling. The rose-gold shine reflects the natural light in the room and give it more depth, while the natural motifs spread across the rest of the space.

Inside this observatory bedroom by sisters Amy Mobley and Katie Norrid of City Hill Design, the Verellen bed is raised up from the ground on a custom platform, emphasizing the importance of the furniture’s role in this space meant for relaxation and rest. It’s wrapped in a striped silk carpet that beckons your touch with a chartreuse mohair sofa at the foot.

Related Story: A Trip to the Lone Star State Inspired the Earthy Textures of This Tennessee New Build

Colorful and collected, this vibrant library by designer Betsy Wentz is not visually quiet. The maximalist space is covered in pattern-mixed Schumacher from ceiling to floor, and the designer pumped up the volume by opting for this triangular table in this tucked away corner.

In designer Amy Switzer’s textural bar lounge—filled with plush chairs, unique artwork, large tassel accessories, and beaded cigarettes—it’s the stone details that really make the room sing. Onyx cornices crown the windows, blue agate inlays decorate the floor, and a banquette which curves around a custom stone and wood table bring a natural coolness to the room that only stone can bring.

#7088. Patek Philippe's Golden Ellipse Reimagines a Design Classic

A watch that celebrates a mathematical ratio for natural harmony, now in verdant green.

Above: Patek Philippe Golden Ellipse watch in white gold, $43,011, patek.com

Few watches make the case for pure design quite as convincingly as the Patek Philippe Golden Ellipse. Introduced in 1968, its distinctive shape was conceived around the golden ratio, the mathematical proportion found throughout the natural world and long associated with harmony in art and architecture. Neither round nor angular, the curves of the Ellipse have an elemental quality that elevates its simplicity. Despite origins rooted in Renaissance mathematics, the watch also has an unmistakable groovy ’60s elegance.

For 2026 Patek Philippe returns to that formula with the Golden Ellipse (Reference 5738G-001). A larger case gives the watch more presence on the wrist, and at just a hair under 6mm thick, it retains the sleek profile that has always been central to its appeal. Inside is Patek’s ultra-thin self-winding caliber 240, equipped with a 22K gold mini-rotor.

The Golden Ellipse’s ode to nature continues through its use of color. The olive-green sunburst dial—and coordinating calfskin strap—recalls a shade found almost everywhere in the landscape, from leaves and moss to deep forest tones, shifting from an earthy green to brighter grassy flashes as it catches the light. Against the cool white gold case, matching baton markers and slender cheveux-type hands keep the dial remarkably clean.

Nearly six decades after its debut, the Golden Ellipse remains a design classic: mathematics, nature, color, and restraint distilled in one resonant shape.

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#7087. Liberace’s Palm Springs Estate Hits the Market for $6.9 Million—Plus 4 More Luxury Homes for Sale

Also for sale this week: Annette Bening and Warren Beatty’s onetime home and the most expensive house in Charleston.

One of the most legendary entertainers in American history, Liberace dazzled audiences for decades with his over-the-top piano medleys, flamboyant costumes, and cheeky banter. The self-described “Mr. Showmanship” might be synonymous with Las Vegas, where he had a popular residency, but he also had a taste for Palm Springs, owning no fewer than four homes in the California desert oasis throughout his life.

Now, perhaps the most exceptional of those properties, a 1930s Spanish Revival estate—dubbed the Cloisters due to its network of arcades and courtyards—has hit the market for $6.9 million. Elsewhere in Southern California, the longtime Beverly Hills home of Hollywood power couple Annette Bening and Warren Beatty is also newly for sale, asking almost $15 million. And on the East Coast, Lady Gaga’s former penthouse atop Manhattan’s Hit Factory building is available for $7 million, while the most expensive home in Charleston just listed for $35 million. Check them all out below.

Acquired at the height of his career in the late 1960s, Liberace’s walled Spanish Colonial Revival estate in Palm Springs just hit the market for nearly $6.9 million. The famed entertainer turned the 1930s bed-and-breakfast, known as the Cloisters, into an extension of his opulent stage set, brimming with signature candelabras, opulent furnishings, and splashes of gold. He lived on the half-acre property until his death in 1987, after which new owners have completely remodeled the 8,000-square-foot home to bring it back to its original splendor. HGTV host Brett Waterman spearheaded the most recent update, filling the five-bedroom home with handcrafted details and understated furnishings. Perks include a heated in-ground pool, courtyards, and a spa facing the San Jacinto Mountains. It’s listed with Keith Markovitz and Rick Distel of TTK Represents at Compass.

On Wednesday, ELLE Decor exclusively reported that the longtime Beverly Hills home of Warren Beatty and Annette Bening was hitting the market for the first time since they sold it. The Hollywood power couple custom-built the gated residence in 1992 and raised their four kids there, selling it in 2012 to hotelier Jonathan Baker and his wife, Jenny. They spearheaded their own revamp to the tune of $5 million, bringing a contemporary spirit to the interiors of the nine-bedroom compound, which includes a guest house, pool, and tennis court. Its new asking price is $14.995 million.

Last year, a 225-year-old mansion smashed the record for the most expensive home sold in Charleston, fetching a little over $21 million. Now, another estate is vying for the title. The Sword Gate House, located in the South Carolina city’s historic South of Broad neighborhood, just listed for $35 million with Leslie Turner of Maison Real Estate. Revamped by interior designer Cortney Bishop, the 17,140-square-foot mansion dates to the early 1800s and features preserved millwork, exposed brick, and an original mural in the dining room depicting historic Charleston harbor. There are also uncommon perks like a triple-decker piazza, a ballroom, a pool, and an acre of manicured gardens. The whole compound is secured behind preserved wrought-iron gates made by local ironworker Christopher Werner, giving the landmark its moniker.

Forget a “Million Reasons”—we’re looking at $7 million. The New York City penthouse where Lady Gaga once lived just hit the market for nearly $7 million—and she’s not the home’s only celebrity connection. The 3,700-square-foot duplex was also once home to former NBA star Carmelo Anthony and 30 Rock actor Tracy Morgan, per People.

With its organic silhouette, cantilevered roofline, and clever use of space, Leuthold House by Allan Gelbin bears the hallmarks of his mentor, legendary architect Frank Lloyd Wright. Completed in 1966 on five rolling acres in New Canaan, the five-bedroom house spans roughly 5,700 square feet and underwent a restoration by Hobbs Inc. and JMK Architects in 2014. The interiors feature long expanses of glass and mahogany, while the grounds come with a spring-fed pond, a pool, a one-bedroom guest house, and 150 specimen trees. Listed for $6.2 million with John Engel and Melissa Engel of Douglas Elliman, the two-story house is on the market for the first time in 29 years.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#7086. This Playful Florida Raised Ranch Is Proof That Bold Style Can Be Incredibly Timeless

Classic materials and a modern layout read as "traditional with a twist."

How do you turn a straightforward ranch-style home into something exciting and unexpected yet timeless? When a young family of four asked designer James Farmer to do just that for their central Florida abode, he opted for a gut renovation, using his homegrown knowledge of Southern style, years of interior expertise, and pointed enthusiasm to renovate ranches to create a highly functional and striking escape for his clients.

Located in an old, established neighborhood on a lake in Winter Park, Florida, the home was surrounded by mature live oaks and beautiful landscaping. “I would describe the area as old-school Florida with a touch of Mayberry,” says Farmer, referencing the fictional town from The Andy Griffith Show. The home itself was humble and honest—“no hiding its hand at the poker table,” says Farmer.

Alongside Skolfield Homes, Farmer kept the architectural style generally the same, with the most impactful changes being a complete overhaul of the galley kitchen and a relocation of the staircase from the side of the house to the middle, putting it in its deserved place as a focal point of the living room. The new kitchen is what he calls a “glorified galley,” with a sunny breakfast room off to the side that used to be a screened-in porch. He then reinforced the home as a "front door house," ensuring the formal entry space would actually be used rather than passed through by adding a bar perfect for fancy cocktail parties or a casual post-work martini.

For the actual interior, the designer layered various textures and bold patterns, combining them with hallmarks of grand Floridian style, like floral wallpapers, gold details, intricate trim, palm motifs, pecky cypress wallcoverings, and a coastal color palette. The line between indoor and outdoor living is further blurred with trellis details and a selection of timeworn brick and wood flooring. "We really leaned into this in a new way with this project because of its location," he says. "True, classic Florida can be so fun but also timeless, and that was our goal with this project."

Farmer encouraged his clients to splurge only on materials and pieces that'll truly stand the test of time, opting for classic finishes and prints that will age with the young family—an approach he details intimately in his newest book, Home Again: A Return to Gracious Interiors. Ultimately, the decadent interior paired with the modern structural changes naturally created what ended up being Farmer's motto for the home: "Traditional with a twist!"

FAST FACTSDesigner: James Farmer of James Farmer DesignsLocation: Winter Park, FloridaThe Space: A ranch-style home with 4 bedrooms and 3.5 bathrooms

Fabulous cocktail parties start in this formal space before migrating to other parts of the house. With no TV in the space, all eyes are drawn to the bar set behind a custom set of accent chairs by Thibaut, which is where Farmer's clients shake up (not stir) "the best martinis in Florida."

A color palette of peach, robin's egg blue, and butterscotch is celebrated in this room, the designer explains. By painting the ceiling in a blue tone—a trick commonly seen on Southern porches—he gave the room a subtle indoor-outdoor touch commonly seen on Southern porches.

Borrowed light from the kitchen and stairwell shines on the rich velvets and great patterns in this family room. “The color combo of terracotta and emerald with creamy white millwork creates a handsome and elegant palette,” Farmer adds.

Once a screened-in porch, Farmer kept the alfresco feel by covering the walls and ceiling with trelliage panels and outfitting the room with timeless white wicker furniture by Memoire Design (once known as Mainly Baskets Home). Vintage elephant garden stools now stand as whimsical side tables.

“We kept the original corner cabinets and wainscoting but gave it a bit of glam with a custom Gracie Studio wallpaper and lacquered ceiling,” Farmer says of the dining room, which kept its original footprint. The console tables are painted in a lemony yellow hue to match the window treatments and the room's other cheerful elements.

Farmer broke up this completely new, double galley kitchen into sections to make hosting a breeze. On one side of the counter is the seating and hangout zone, while the other side plays host to a functional working kitchen, outfitted with a Wolf range and Sub-Zero fridge. Think of it like traditional with a twist; "These clients are fabulous entertainers, and the island can also make a great buffet for parties," he says. "We like to think that The Golden Girls would thrive in this kitchen… Mid-century meets Maw-Maw!"

Old Chicago brick and palm motifs in the wallpaper make this breakfast hub feel more like a terrace than an interior enclave. Farmer created this room as part of the kitchen expansion—its bay window only enhances the porch-like vibe he wanted. "Sometimes geometry is your best friend," Farmer says, noting that the rug, table, and even shape of the room are octagonal. "You might not notice it, but it makes a moment."

“We were able to move the staircase in this house, which allowed for a more efficient floor plan for this young family,” he explains. “When you are under a major renovation, you can think outside of the box, or sometimes inside the box, just as easily.” Now the stairwell can live as a beautiful moment all its own at the center of the home, rather than pushed to the side.

The daughter was sleeping in a crib when Farmer first started working on his clients' house, so he wanted to design a room that could grow with her (by the time the project wrapped, she was in her big girl bed). The pink trellis wallpaper by Schumacher perfectly rides the line between feminine and elevated.

Touches of bamboo, palm, and pecky cypress introduce that old Florida feel, reminiscent of family vacations to the family's favorite spots in the Sunshine State, without being too on the nose.

“An interior design trick that is so old-school, but still so fun, is using a drapery fabric for wall covering and vice versa,” Farmer explains. “We were able to bring that from the primary bedroom to the primary bathroom.”

James Farmer is a Southern author, interior designer, and speaker known for his ability to create beautifully familiar and welcoming homes. To James, a home engages all of the senses—the pleasantries of colors and materials, the feel of the doorknob warmed by the sun, the smell of fresh laundry, the sound of the closing door, and the taste of supper on the table. Through his design firm, James works with clients to truly create a home.

Projects by James Farmer Designs can be found from the countryside of Connecticut to James's home state of Georgia. Though his work can be found throughout the country, James proudly has built his business in his hometown of Perry, Georgia.

#7085. Designers Say "Cook's Tables" Are About to Replace Kitchen Islands in 2027

Their versatility is just part of their appeal.

Pictured Above: A kitchen designed by Amy Sklar.

For years, oversized kitchen islands have been the be-all and end-all, but lately, designers and homeowners are gravitating toward something a bit smaller: a cook’s table.

Unlike built-in islands that can sometimes dominate a kitchen and cover most of the available floor space, cook’s tables bring a lot of flexibility to the room. Because they’re freestanding, you can move them around depending on your needs (especially if they’re on wheels), whether that’s for prepping meals, hanging out with your morning coffee, or helping kids with homework after school. They also tend to be narrower, making them the perfect solution for small kitchens.

The other benefit is the big charm factor they add. They’re often wooden, with traditional-looking design elements, like turned legs and curvy silhouettes. Cooks tables can also frequently be found in vintage and antique stores, so they’re a great way to work a bit of charm and patina into an otherwise utilitarian space.

As kitchens move away from ultra-sleek and minimal, pieces with warmth and character are becoming more and more popular, and a cook’s table is the perfect example of that. If you’re considering adding one to your own home, learn what to look for—and how to style it—below.

One of the biggest reasons cook’s tables are trending right now is that they soften the overall look and feel of a kitchen. “A traditional island can dominate a kitchen,” says designer Amy Sklar. Because cook’s tables don’t have a bulky built-in base, they feel visually lighter and less imposing, especially in kitchens where space is limited.

Designers also point to the emotional shift happening in homes post-pandemic. “The pandemic reshaped the way we live at home, placing greater value on spaces designed for connection and togetherness,” says designer Becky Ducsik of The Phinery. “Nowhere is this more apparent than in the kitchen, where homeowners are reimagining how they gather and entertain.”

Unlike traditional islands that are packed with appliances and storage, cook’s tables create a more communal focal point centered around gathering and spending time together. That relaxed atmosphere is part of what makes them feel so appealing. Designer Cynthia Lananh notes that cook’s tables strike a balance between practicality and personality, functioning as prep space, dining space, and entertaining hub all at once. “I love a kitchen island that looks like a piece of furniture,” she says, adding that the look feels more casual, approachable, and timeless than a standard built-in island.

While cook’s tables can absolutely make a statement in a large kitchen, designers say they’re especially effective in kitchens with tighter footprints or galley-style layouts. Because they take up less visual and physical space than a traditional island, they can help smaller kitchens feel more open and airy.

That said, larger kitchens can benefit from them too, just in a different way. “With the right vintage find or custom-crafted piece, it can anchor the room and become a striking centerpiece that adds both function and timeless appeal,” says Ducsik.

She recommends using cook’s tables as the focal point in kitchens where storage and appliances can be concentrated along the perimeter. Since seating typically wraps around all sides, she suggests leaving at least four to five feet of clearance around the table to maintain a comfortable flow.

For homeowners who want the look but don’t have space for a freestanding table in the center of the room, Ducsik also suggests integrating one as a peninsula extension at the end of cabinetry for a similar communal feel.

Unlike built-in islands, cook’s tables offer a level of flexibility that’s hard to beat. Since they’re freestanding, they can be moved or adapted more easily over time—a feature designers say is especially useful for entertaining.

Sklar points out that cook’s tables can create a flexible landing zone within a kitchen and are ideal for holidays or large gatherings when extra room is needed. “This is perfect for holidays and large gatherings where you need even more space,” Lananh says. She recommends adding casters if the table will double as both a prep and dining space, making it easier to move around as needed.

Scale is one of the most important things to get right: The table should feel proportional to the room while still leaving enough space to comfortably move around it. Height matters, too. Sklar notes that many vintage tables sit lower than standard counter height, so homeowners may need to modify them slightly with casters or leg extenders to make them more functional for prep work.

As for materials, wood remains the go-to choice for warmth and character. “Durable woods (like oak, maple, or walnut) are popular for their warmth, strength, and character,” says Lananh. “Reclaimed wood adds rustic charm, whereas metal or butcher block tops offer a more durable, industrial aesthetic.”

Ducsik echoes those sentiments and suggests echoing the table’s wood tones throughout the kitchen with details like open shelving, cutting boards, or serving pieces to create a cohesive look.

Lastly, focus on shape. Softer corners and curves can make the space feel more intimate, while rectangular tables can maximize seating and prep space. Ultimately, the goal is for the piece to feel like furniture, not cabinetry. And that’s exactly what makes cook’s tables so appealing in the first place.

#7083. Massie moves to force Hegseth impeachment vote

Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., conducts a news conference outside the Capitol on Monday. (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., took to the House floor Monday to unveil a resolution that would impeach Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, and a vote on the issue could occur by Wednesday.

A draft resolution that Massie read aloud on the House floor on Monday accused Hegseth of eight violations of law or the Constitution, including that he violated the 1973 War Powers Resolution in his prosecution of the Iran war and acted illegally in military campaigns in Venezuela, Yemen, the Caribbean Sea and the eastern Pacific Ocean.

Massie's resolution also charges Hegseth with disregarding protection of civilians in wartime and suppressing the free speech of a U.S. senator.

Massie announced his intention to file the resolution as a matter of the privileges of the House.

Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., will now have to rule as to whether the resolution constitutes a proper question of the privileges of the House. There is precedent from prior instances when impeachment was found to be valid as it relates to the House’s constitutionally granted powers.

He must schedule a vote to occur within two legislative days. Assuming that the speaker rules that Massie's resolution is privileged, the House could vote on the resolution itself, or on a motion to table it or refer it to committee.

If the House were to impeach Hegseth under this process, it would mark a historical anomaly. According to the Congressional Research Service, recent attempts to impeach an official as a matter of the privileges of the House have not resulted in approval of the resolution. In cases in which an official has been impeached, the House has nearly always chosen to conduct an investigation first.

In an interview on Oct. 26, Massie put the chances of his resolution being adopted at 50-50.

In comments to reporters outside the Capitol after his floor remarks, Massie said he suspected some Republicans may back his resolution but he indicated he has neither asked for nor received any such assurances from any of them.

He predicted there could be some GOP support in the Senate if Hegseth is impeached and it goes to trial. He noted that if the trial does not start until 2027, any Republicans who might vote to convict Hegseth would presumably be joined by most if not all of a more sizeable Senate Democratic caucus.

Massie's odds of success for his resolution may now be greater than his previous 50-50 prediction given the results of the Nov. 3 midterm elections, which gave Democrats control over both chambers in the next Congress — a vote motivated largely by opposition to the Iran war and its inflationary impact.

The questions now are, first, how many House Republicans will defy President Donald Trump and vote to impeach Hegseth; and, secondly, how many incumbents who lost their reelection bids on Nov. 3, a cohort that is disproportionately Republican, might be absent for the impeachment vote?

Massie said some Republicans who lost their reelection bids may feel freer to vote to impeach Hegseth. And he also said Republicans who were reelected and will have to face voters in 2028 may also think about how their vote on an impeachment resolution would reverberate politically.

"It's going to be everybody's conscience, and then they'll have to live with that vote," Massie said. "And they're going to have to live with that vote two years from now if they're not part of the retirement caucus. And if they were part of the retirement caucus, what do they have to lose?"

Asked why he did not bring articles of impeachment against President Donald Trump, Massie said: "I think the president needs to see one of — this is a metaphor — one of his horses shot out from under him, and then maybe he'll he will see things differently."

If the House does vote on Massie’s resolution, Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who is expected to become the House speaker in the next Congress, said on ABC’s “This Week” on Sunday that he would be a “hard yes” on it.

Hegseth has “got to go,” Jeffries said. “The so-called secretary of Defense has abused his power, undermined our national security and engineered this reckless and costly war of choice in Iran, which has skyrocketed prices on the American people and made the country less safe.”

Most if not all House Democrats are expected to agree.

Last week, Rep. Adam Smith, D-Wash., the ranking member of the Armed Services Committee and its likely chairman in the next Congress, indicated in an interview with The New Republic that he would support Massie’s impeachment resolution, and he predicted it would be adopted.

Referring to Massie’s measure, Smith said, “By every estimation, it will pass and he will be impeached. And off that goes to the Senate. Then the Senate is legally required to spend all of their time dealing with that until it’s done with.”

"Every second Pete Hegseth remains at the top of the Pentagon, our servicemembers are at risk," Rep. Jason Crow, D-Colo., a senior member of the Armed Services Committee, said in a post on X on Sunday.

Also on Monday, Everett B. Kelley, the national president of the American Federation of Government Employees, a union that represents some 820,000 federal and D.C. government employees, sent a letter to House members urging a “yes” vote on Massie's Hegseth impeachment resolution.

The letter cited the risks Kelley said the Iran war has posed to the lives of the Defense Department's uniformed and civilian personnel, and the missive noted Hegseth's stripping of workplace rights and protections for the department's civilians.

"By adopting Rep. Massie’s resolution, the House can begin the long overdue bipartisan effort to reclaim its constitutional powers and authority, restore checks and balances, and take responsibility for the lives of military personnel and civilian employees that Mr. Hegseth’s capricious leadership has jeopardized," Kelley wrote.

Massie announced in mid-September his intention to file an impeachment resolution against Hegseth. But Johnson adjourned the House before the vote would have occurred.

Massie’s new resolution is substantially the same as his earlier one, with the same eight articles of impeachment.

The first three of these alleges that Hegseth’s conduct of the Iran war violated the War Powers Resolution.

Massie's measure says Hegseth prosecuted the Iran war without congressional authorization and in the absence of an attack on the United States or an imminent threat.

Further, the measure alleges that Hegseth continued the war despite the House and Senate adopting in June a concurrent war powers resolution calling for the withdrawal of U.S. forces from the fight.

And, the resolution says, Hegseth continued the Iran war after the expiration of the War Powers Resolution’s 60-day termination period and any potential 30-day withdrawal period.

Massie’s measure further asserts that Hegseth:

The House has only voted twice to impeach a member of the Cabinet. In 1876, Secretary of War William Belknap was impeached over allegations of corruption, and his abrupt resignation likely contributed to his acquittal in the Senate.

In 2024, the House voted 214-213 to impeach Alejandro Mayorkas, then-secretary of Homeland Security, for “willful and systemic refusal to comply with the law” and “breach of public trust.” The Senate dispensed with the charges without a trial.

#7080. Designers Say These 9 Unexpected Bed Styling Tricks Can Wake Up Your Whole Bedroom

These pillow ideas make your bed feel intentional, collected, and joyful.

A standard pillow setup—two king pillows against the headboard, with two standard pillows in the front, and a square accent pillow in the center—is a snooze. Designers are treating the bed as an opportunity to showcase a bit of flair. Not to mention awaken the entire bedroom.

What kinds of changes are happening? Throw and accent pillows are now treated as if they are fashion accessories. We’re seeing everything from edgy studs to romantic ruffles in contrasting colors to graphic lines that hypnotize. In fact, mismatched pieces work great and can feel “intentional, collected, and completely joyful,” says designer Betsy Wentz.

Here, nine unconventional ways to play with your bed pillow arrangements.

If the rest of your bedroom is neutral, designers are saying it’s OK to go more bold with your bed pillows. In the above space, it’s impossible not to notice the hypnotizing hue of Yves Klein blue pillows on a striped duvet cover.

“There’s something about that particular blue,” says designer Kate Anlyan, founder of Yasu Home. “It’s so vivid and almost electric, but it also has a depth to it. I love how it cuts through all the other tones without feeling disconnected from the rest of the room.” The hue works as a counterpoint to the wood paneling in the background and provides a balance to the visual weight of the black headboard.

The interconnection between the contrasting stripes on the square pillows and the round velvet ball feels both fun and sophisticated, says designer Beth Diana Smith. The stripes feel especially striking, bringing a sense of energy and definition to the space, when set against the moody purple-brown Benjamin Moore's Cinnamon Slate wall color. To keep the rest of the bedding from competing, Smith tames the room with a muted light gray coverlet.

“Match up the headboard, throws, lamps and artwork with your throw pillows to balance out color throughout your room,” says designer Renée Gaddis. The encapsulated look creates instant cohesion. Starting with a bold hue, like a mustard yellow, will make your job easier because it has personality from the start.

Faux fur is a classic way to add rich texture, but you don’t need to reserve it for a château. Interior designer Mialani Collard uses a neutral linen palette and tactile embellishments that sandwich the fluff to make it less prominent, allowing the bed to work even in tropical climates like Florida.

When you want a home with casual living as the main objective, fill surfaces in a way that doesn't look too pristine or orderly. Interior designer Tyson Ness of Studio Ness mixed different patterns and colors for the bedroom pillows so there’s no “right way” to make the bed after it’s been used.

“The different combinations create a slightly different look each time, keeping the overall feeling light, airy, and totally casual,” Ness adds.

Choosing pillows that match the bedding is easy, but Cate Gutter, owner and principal designer of CWG Design, prefers to add a bit of je ne sais quoi when she styles the bed.

“This bedroom is tucked away in a mountain home here in North Carolina, and we wanted it to have a playful, nostalgic feel reminiscent of summer camp,” Gutter says. “We carried that inspiration into the pillow designs, giving them an almost handmade, craft-like quality that feels whimsical.”

The oversized pillows are made in Imogen Heath's Arty Check, a cheerful yellow check finished with a fuzzy green fringe. In front of it, a smaller bolster is a playful take on a traditional shape, but the muted, multicolored stripe with a contrasting ruffled flange bolsters extra personality. The added texture breaks up the monotony of solid linens.

“Studs are an unexpected detail on bed pillows because they’re so versatile,” says Sean Carter, senior interior designer at Sapphire Pear. “They look just as great on a neutral fabric as they do with bold colors, prints, and patterns.” The metallic element adds a little edge without overpowering the overall design.

Wentz is no stranger when it comes to combining patterns and layering different scales in the same color family. Look for prints with their own personality that won’t compete with each other.

“The smaller confetti and stick stripe pillows give your eye a place to rest against that big graphic headboard, so they feel like friends, not strangers,” Wentz says.

Pillows gone wild can still balance restraint. Creating serious contrast—in pattern direction—will step up your bedscaping game.

“The geometric patterns in the pillows complement the strong linear elements,” says interior designer Nicole Johnson. “The vertical wood slats draw the eye upward, while the horizontal lines of the bed and furnishings provide balance.”

Linear patterns in the pillows at a smaller scale create a picture-in-picture effect that won’t overwhelm the overall design.

#7078. What Watch Will Robert Pattinson Wear as James Bond? It's Complicated.

Timepieces are almost as much a part of 007’s wardrobe as his dinner jackets.

Perhaps you've heard? There's a new James Bond in town!

Robert Pattinson has been announced as the next 007, and while most of the speculation has understandably focused on how the former vampire/current Batman might approach Britain's foremost secret agent, watch aficionados are asking another question: What will he wear on his wrist?

Watches, after all, are almost as much a part of Bond's wardrobe as his dinner jackets, Aston Martins, and vodka martinis. Sean Connery famously wore a Rolex Submariner, Roger Moore experimented with Seiko's digital watches, and Omega has been 007's watch of choice since Pierce Brosnan strapped on a trusty Seamaster in GoldenEye in 1995. That brand remains, of course, a paid partner of the franchise.

Yesterday's reveal showed no watch on the newly anointed 007's wrist, and Pattinson's own taste in watches is rather different from the rugged, sporty timepieces traditionally associated with Bond.

In fact, he doesn't seem particularly keen on big watches at all.

"Whenever a watch gets too thick and it keeps your cuff up, I don't really understand the appeal," he told one interviewer as recently September. "A lot of guys really like that, but I don't think I can really pull that off. I need to only have a half reveal."

And when asked what he looks for in a watch, he was even more specific.

"I definitely like really thin watches. That's the main thing. In general, I would probably prefer a leather strap."

Which is quite a contrast with the substantial divers we've become accustomed to seeing on Bond's wrist.

The fact is, until recently, Pattinson wasn't much of a watch enthusiast at all. That changed at this year's Oscars, when he wore a then-unreleased rose-gold Jaeger-LeCoultre Master Control Chronomètre Perpetual Calendar. Other actors apparently began asking him about it at the afterparty, and something clicked.

He subsequently became an ambassador for Jaeger-LeCoultre, although his relationship with the brand goes back much further. As a teenager, he borrowed his mother's Reverso to impress his schoolmates. More recently, he's revealed that Bruce Wayne will wear a Reverso in the next Batman film.

On screen, Pattinson's watch choices have been interesting. He wore a Chanel J12 in David Cronenberg's Cosmopolis, while Christopher Nolan's Tenet, in which he played a mysterious secret agent, featured a Bond-adjacent Omega De Ville.

Meanwhile, the announcement has prompted some entertaining activity on Instagram. Within hours of Pattinson being named Bond, Jaeger-LeCoultre posted pictures of its new ambassador wearing the Master Control Chronomètre Perpetual Calendar. Omega, meanwhile, posted an image of its James Bond Seamaster. And today, Jaeger-LeCoultre has been back with more Pattinson pictures.

It's a little social-media tit-for-tat, although there's no reason the two relationships can't coexist. For now, at least, there's a straightforward distinction: Pattinson can wear whatever he likes. Bond has his own watch.

#7076. I Just Finished My Kitchen Renovation, and This “Hidden” Island Feature Is the One Upgrade I’d Recommend to Everyone

It’s made my cook space so much more functional.

I’m always noticing something cool in someone else’s home and thinking, “Oh, I need that, too.” That happened last year when my family stayed with my in-laws. It was my first time seeing their newly renovated kitchen, and after a quick tour, one small detail caught my eye: the pop-up outlet in the kitchen island.

It was simple, practical, and exactly the kind of feature I wanted in my own kitchen. When we recently renovated our cook space, it went to the top of our wish list—and it has indeed been a game-changer with how we use the area.

Ahead, here’s why I love having a pop-up outlet, what to consider before installing one, and a few other kitchen gadgets that you might want to consider adding to your next upgrade.

Like the name suggests, a pop-up outlet is basically an electrical outlet that hides inside your countertop until you need it. Most models have a top you press or pull, and the outlet rises up so you can plug in your blender, mixer, or whatever else you're using. When you're done, you push it back down and it's out of sight.

There are a few different versions, including ones that pop up manually and others that rise with the push of a button. Some, like the one we have, include USB ports or wireless charging built in. The outlet connects to power underneath the countertop, so it's something you'll want to plan for before your new counters go in.

Like pretty much any kitchen gadget, there are a range of options available from budget to high end. We opted for something mid-tier that still included the main functions we wanted like the additional USB ports and the charging capability on the top. If you’re a chronic “let your phone get down to one or two percent before finally charging it” person like me, I highly recommend this.

There also are some that will go nearly fully flush with the countertop, and can even have part of the countertop material on the top of it to look seamless. We did not go this route, but if you prefer the look of a flawless countertop, this might be the way to go.

We put ours in the corner of our kitchen where it’s almost entirely hidden by decor. That corner of the kitchen was otherwise without any spots to plug in appliances, so this fixed multiple problems by having electricity access while remaining discreet.

You can really put them anywhere you want, but keep in mind where you’ll need extra power accessibility and where it won’t be an eyesore, if that’s a concern. I’m also a big fan of in-drawer charging outlets.

There was some back-and-forth about adding this pop-up outlet to our kitchen remodel budget. The outlet itself isn’t break-the-bank expensive, but there were extra expenses that went with it, like hiring an electrician and paying to have a hole cut in the new countertop.

Though we decided it was worth the expense, here are some of the pros and cons you should consider if you’re on the fence.

I’m always so impressed by kitchens that integrate small, helpful gadgets like this one into their design. Here are a few other popular additions that we either made or were thinking about adding to our renovation.

Maybe it’s just a me thing, but I feel like the harder it is to find an appliance or trash can, the fancier a kitchen is. The pull-out trash can was integrated into our kitchen peninsula, and we purchased a more expensive panel-ready dishwasher so it looked like a regular cabinet. An integrated refrigerator was almost twice as expensive as a regular one, so we did not go that route.

With the way our kitchen is laid out, we unfortunately have two awkward corner cabinets. For one, we upgraded with blind corner cabinet hardware, which was well worth it.

We did not integrate a bottle rinser into our current kitchen design, but we did in our last two kitchens and loved them. It got so much use when our two kids were babies, since we could quickly rinse out bottles. They’re also great for wine drinkers or anyone who hosts a lot of parties.

Admittedly, this one is controversial, as some designers don’t like this feature. But personally, we love the look and it’s next on our “cool kitchen wish list.”

He has written for multiple home improvement magazines and websites, drawing on his own experience renovating homes with his wife to create practical, inspiring content for homeowners and DIY enthusiasts alike.

#7074. 5 Ways Warmth Has Shaped Interior Design in 2026, According to Experts

Warmth is more than a palette—it’s the secret to a home you never want to leave.

After years of cool neutrals and minimalist interiors, there’s been a shift toward warmer, more inviting spaces that feel layered, personal, and lived-in.

“Warmth has become more than just a color palette or a particular material. It's about spaces that feel authentic, comfortable, and reflective of the people who live in them,” says Laura Gerber, founder of ODE Design.

At the end of the day, a warm home should feel inviting and effortless, with materials and details that make people want to spend time there. If you’re looking to add some warmth to your home, take inspiration from these designer-approved ideas.

Wood’s organic grain brings instant warmth to both modern and traditional homes, adding depth without making a space feel overly formal.

“We’re incorporating more white oak, walnut, and rift-cut cabinetry than we have in years, along with wood ceilings and paneled walls,” explains Rachel Simpson, senior interior designer at Revive Design and Renovation.

There’s been a shift from stark white countertops toward natural quartzite, travertine, and marble with warmer veining. These materials have natural variation and movement that bring character into a space.

“Honed finishes, in particular, feel softer and more organic than high-polish surfaces. They allow the beauty of the stone to speak for itself without feeling overly formal or glossy,” explains Simpson.

These days, people really want their homes to feel like a sanctuary.

“In a recent project, the client envisioned a grown-up treehouse nestled among the evergreens. We wanted to create a warm yet contemporary mountain retreat that felt luxurious but also comfortable and welcoming,” says Charles Greenfeld, founder and principal designer at Ethan Charles Design.

Rather than relying on one or two dramatic statement pieces, the interiors were designed to be experienced as a whole, with each detail contributing to that warm aesthetic.

Creating warmth is really about creating layers of thoughtfully curated design elements. This helps to create a home you want to enter and never leave.

“Well-proportioned rugs, textures on walls, loads of art, draperies, and lighting overhead and at eye level are just some of the things we can add to our spaces to make them feel cozy and accessible,” says Ashley Powell, cofounder and interior designer of Roseberry Allen.

Designers have lately been drawn to rooms where the colors are close to one another, but the materials do different things.

“A woven rug, a textured wall, and a piece of wood with a visible grain each catch the light differently,” says Jess Klein, founder and principal of Jess Klein Studio.

That variation adds visual depth and makes the room feel warmer and more inviting, even when the color palette is understated.

#7072. These MacBook Deals Are Good Enough to Shop Before Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Considering the price of tech, we’ll gladly take $250 off a new MacBook.

If there’s one Apple product line where the recent price hikes hit the hardest, it’s the MacBook. Rising RAM and storage costs have pushed laptop prices up across the board, which makes scoring a new MacBook on sale all the more mission critical.

As luck would have it, Black Friday deals on Apple tech are already ramping up on Amazon, including discounts on MacBooks. While there’s always the chance these deals might continue to deepen in the next week, the current discounts are nothing to sneeze at if you can’t wait that long to shop.

And if budget is your primary concern, we’re of the opinion that the MacBook Neo is a worthy buy, even if it’s only $50 off right now.

The best MacBook Air deal you’ll find right now is this $250 discount on the 15-inch model. With this laptop, you’ll get Apple’s M5 chip in a fanless, 3.3-pound frame that handles heavy multitasking and light video editing without breaking a sweat. Base storage doubled to 512 GB this year—a great upgrade for storing more apps, photos, and files—and the 15.3-inch Liquid Retina display is sharp and vivid. You also get a 12 MP Center Stage camera that’s great on video calls, WiFi 7 for fast downloads, and up to 18 hours of battery, enough for a busy workday and then some. It won’t replace a MacBook Pro for serious creative work or gaming, but for school, business, and general use, it’s the one to get.

Deals on 13-inch models aren’t as steep—yet. But we expect that to change in a week’s time.

#7070. The One Kitchen Feature Designers Always Splurge on—and Why It’s Worth It

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: Meghan Jay selected a brass chandelier with ribbed glass orbs to modernize this traditional kitchen.

The kitchen is the hardest-working room in your house, but it’s also one of the most expensive spaces to renovate. Depending on the size and scope of your redo, reimagining this space can cost up to six figures, so it’s important to be wise about allocating your budget. While some people may overspend on high-end appliances or really luxurious countertops, you should never overlook the room’s lighting. Turns out, that’s where designers always splurge in the kitchen.

“Lighting has such a powerful ability to change how a room feels,” explains Meghan Jay, an interior designer in Winnetka, Illinois. “You can have the most beautiful cabinetry, stone, and hardware, but if the lighting isn't right, the space can feel flat or even cold.”

Meghan Meehan of Hudson East Design in New Jersey agrees and says the right fixtures can provide height, dimensionality, and lots of mood to the space. “Don't think of lighting as the finishing touch,” she adds. “Think of it as one of the main design elements that makes the entire kitchen.”

Ahead, here’s why spending a little more on your kitchen lighting is worth it—and exactly how much you should budget for.

Every kitchen deserves to glow at full wattage, but it will cost you. After all, illuminating a kitchen isn’t as simple as adding a budget-friendly portable lamp and calling it a day: The best and brightest kitchens usually include several sources such as sconces, recessed ceiling fixtures, and under-cabinet lighting to illuminate meal-prepping zones.

“It needs to provide the right illumination for cooking and everyday tasks, but it also creates warmth and ambiance when you're entertaining or gathering with family,” Meehan explains. As a rule of thumb, she recommends homeowners allocate at least 8 to 12 percent of their kitchen renovation budget to lighting.

That said, anyone who wants to wow with some statement fixtures might wind up spending more. Jay, for example, suggests setting aside $3,000 to $7,000 for decorative wares such as pendant lights above an island.

Admittedly, spending thousands of dollars on lighting is bound to elicit some sticker shock, and some people may wonder if it’s worth it. However, Lauren Wodicka insists it’s worth the investment. “Unlike a sofa, rug, or an appliance, lighting doesn’t experience the same daily wear and tear,” the Northern Virginia designer reasons. “A beautiful, well-made fixture can last for generations and become the focal point of a room.”

Oftentimes, less expensive fixtures can read cheap. “You may notice overly shiny or artificial-looking brass finishes, visible seams, thin metal, or cheaper-looking glass,” Jay says. “Higher-quality fixtures often have more substantial materials, beautiful patinas, and thoughtful details that become more apparent the closer you are.”

If you want to get the best bang for your buck, devise a shopping strategy. Santa Monica-based designer Christine Markatos Lowe first selects functional lighting and then spends the rest of her budget on “decorative pieces with personality.” But make no mistake, a personable light fixture should still have staying power: Lowe often reaches for timeless silhouettes from retailers like Urban Electric and Ann-Morris Lighting and is a sucker for details like handblown glass or “beautifully finished metals.”

“The key to finding the right balance between statement lighting and a cohesive kitchen design is to make sure every element feels intentional,” Lowe explains. “It’s all about allowing each material or element to have its moment without overwhelming the space.”Wodicka—who calls Soane, Hudson Valley Lighting, and Visual Comfort her go-tos—says less is more. “Not every kitchen island needs two or three lighting pendants,” she explains. “Sometimes one beautiful fixture can make a stronger statement.”

Designers often say lighting is the jewelry of a room, so layering different materials, sizes, and finishes adds more visual intrigue. Wodicka wants kitchen lights to complement each other—and the fixtures in surrounding rooms—but they don’t have to be identical. “A light fixture doesn't necessarily have to match the cabinet hardware or plumbing fixtures,” Jay adds. “I often prefer mixing finishes to create a more collected, layered look.”

If you want to keep your fixtures cohesive—without identical finishes—stick with a particular style. For example, a sconce with pleated fabric shades will look out of place next to a midcentury-style pendant that looks like it belongs on the set of Mad Men.

For the perfect finishing touch, Meehan recommends putting all lights on dimmers, which is a simple detail that can make a huge difference. “This allows for functional lighting when cooking, but softer light to warm the space when you are entertaining,” she adds. And, if a small detail can make your investment work even harder, even better.
