#7067. High-Gloss Furniture Will Be Everywhere in 2027—Here's How Designers Make It Feel Fresh

A single lacquered piece can make a neutral room feel instantly more interesting.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

After years of quiet luxury, soft neutrals, and interiors that favor understated details, a little drama feels like a very welcome change. High-gloss furniture is making a comeback, bringing shine, color, and a bit of personality back into our homes. Whether it's a lacquered accent table, a sculptural console, or a statement cabinet with a reflective finish, these pieces have a way of catching your eye and making even the simplest room feel more interesting. And honestly, who says every piece of furniture has to blend into the background?

The look also ties into Neo Deco, a modern take on Art Deco that brings together glamorous materials, bold silhouettes, and a fresh approach to decorating. But you don't need to turn your living room into a scene from The Great Gatsby to get in on it—a single glossy piece can add just the right amount of contrast to a neutral space, while mixing lacquer with natural wood, textured upholstery, or warm metallic accents keeps things feeling layered and livable.

Ahead, designers share how to bring high-gloss furniture into your home and embrace a little more shine without going over the top.

Not ready to go all-in on the high-gloss trend? An accent table is your low-commitment, high-impact way in. Tucked beside your favorite armchair or pulled up next to the sofa, a lacquered side table brings just enough shine to make the whole corner feel a little more interesting. The best part is the contrast, so have fun mixing finishes. Carol Alda, interior designer at Z Gallerie, loves pairing a glossy table with a “velvet or textured woven chair.” Just make sure the table sits at a comfortable height next to the chair so you can actually use it for your coffee (or cocktail).

Color is another way to make the piece feel right at home. Ali Esmaili, design expert and owner of Esmaili Rugs, suggests pulling the shade of your accent table from a patterned rug, which creates an easy connection between the two. An oxblood, emerald, or classic black table brings plenty of Neo Deco attitude, especially in a sculptural shape. It's a small swap, but one that can give even the most neutral living room a welcome dose of personality.

A little high-gloss feels right at home in a bar or entertaining area, where a touch of drama is practically part of the assignment. A lacquered bar credenza or sideboard in a saturated shade can turn your glassware and favorite bottles into a whole display moment.

Jennifer Fitzpatrick, founder of Studio J & Co., explains that high-gloss finishes bring extra energy to a room by reflecting daylight and catching the flicker of candles and lamps in the evening. In a bar area, that means your decanters, glassware, and cocktail accessories get their own little spotlight. Fitzpatrick recommends pairing high-gloss pieces with unlacquered brass, fluted glass, and textured textiles.

The coffee table is already the unofficial centerpiece of the living room, so why not give it a little extra attention? A lacquered version in a rich color or an unexpected shape can instantly make the space feel more interesting. And don't worry about all that shine feeling too formal. Fitzpatrick says today's high-gloss pieces are all about sculptural shapes, from curved profiles to clean geometric forms. A lacquered cocktail table can also catch the glow of your lamps in the evening, adding a little atmosphere without visually crowding your seating arrangement.

To keep the look feeling relaxed, mix that reflective surface with pieces that have plenty of texture. Esmaili loves pairing high-gloss furniture with vintage rugs, noting that “the gloss adds depth, movement and a touch of theatre.” The contrast between a shiny coffee table and a well-loved rug with plenty of pattern and texture gives the room a more layered, lived-in feel. Add a soft sectional and a few simple accessories, and you've got a statement piece that still feels right at home.

#7063. Who Is Daddy Franklin From ‘The Body’? For Starters, Nnamdi Asomugha Is Kerry Washington’s Husband

Daddy Franklin is married to Olivia Pope?

If you watched The Body in one sitting and are now fully obsessed with it, you’re not alone!! The horrifying coming-of-age series is scary good, and while you may not be familiar with the actor who plays Father Samuel Franklin (aka Daddy Franklin), you definitely know his very famous wife. As it turns out, Nnamdi Asomugha has been married to Kerry Washington for over 13 years now, having fallen in love after watching Kerry perform in Race on Broadway in 2010. Kerry later confirmed the story during a 2018 interview with Marie Claire, saying, “The last time I did theater, it completely transformed my life. That’s where I met my husband.” Cute!!!

Though things got off to a rocky start (Kerry apparently told Nnamdi she didn’t date athletes, and Nnamdi told Kerry he didn’t date actors), the two got together anyway, and just about three years later in June 2013, they tied the knot in a super low-key ceremony. According to their marriage license, Kerry and Nnamdi said “I do” in Hailey, Idaho, where Nnamdi’s sister officiated and Kerry’s parents served as witnesses. Since then, the couple has welcomed two kids together—Isabelle Amarachi, born in 2014, and Caleb Kelechi, born in 2016—and are seemingly thriving outside of the spotlight. But we think it’s time to learn a lil bit more about Nnamdi, don’t you?

Here’s everything you should know about the man behind Daddy Franklin, including how he rose to fame as a former NFL cornerback and what he’s up to next.

After growing up in Louisiana in a Nigerian Igbo household, Nnamdi attended the University of California, Berkeley, where he played for the California Golden Bears football team. Soon after graduating, Nnamdi was drafted in the first round of the 2003 NFL Draft by the Oakland Raiders, which is very impressive.

Over his 11-year professional football career, Nnamdi also played for the Philadelphia Eagles and the San Francisco 49ers, and in December 2013, the cornerback signed a one-day contract with Oakland so he could retire as a Raider. I love to see a loyal man!

According to Nnamdi, the decision to retire at 32 was difficult, but it still felt like the right time for him. “Yeah, I felt that it was a good time, but it didn’t make it any easier,” he told UPROXX. “It’s a game that 'til this day I miss, and I miss playing. It’s not that I want to go play again, but I miss so many aspects of it; being around the team, being around the coaching, that calm before the storm.”

Nnamdi first caught the acting bug all the way back in 2008, when he landed the role of “Party Guest with Wife” on the CW sitcom The Game. (Remember: There are no small roles, only small actors!!) He then caught the eye of director Peter Berg on the set of a Nike commercial shoot in 2009, who was impressed by the athlete’s ease in front of the camera. From there, Nnamdi went on to play small roles in Friday Night Lights, Leverage, and Cubed before making his on-screen feature film debut in 2012’s Fire with Fire.

Since retiring from the NFL, Nnamdi has fully committed himself to his acting career with starring roles in the films Crown Heights, Sylvie’s Love, The Good Nurse, The Knife, and—most recently—The Body. He’s also served as a producer on several of those projects as well.

Despite all his success, Nnamdi still remains as modest as ever, avoiding social media and press to instead focus on his craft. “I’m really bad at self-promotion. I know that about myself,” he previously explained to Bleacher Report back in 2017. “People told me all of the time, ‘You could be such a big star if you just talked about yourself more,’ but I’m not good at that. It’s always been about team.”

During a 2018 interview with Today, Kerry corrected a host when they alluded to her two children, saying, “I’m a mother of three." The reason: Nnamdi has a now-teenage daughter from another relationship, who Kerry has helped raise alongside her two biological children. And just like with Isabelle and Caleb, the couple keeps their eldest far from the paparazzi, so very little is known about her.

As Kerry joked to People, “I have moments where I take pictures or videos of one of my three amazing kids and I wanna post it online, and I tend to just send it to my parents or my shrink instead.”

Philanthropy is important to Nnamdi, which is why he created the Asomugha Foundation in 2005 at the very beginning of his career.

The foundation has two main programs: Orphans and Widows in Need (which provides food, shelter, medicine, vocational training, literacy efforts, and scholarships to widows and orphans in Nigeria) and Asomugha College Tour for Scholars (which takes selected students from Bay Area and Los Angeles area high schools on college tours across the country).

Though Kerry and Nnamdi are both actors and producers, it isn’t often that they work together on creative projects. Back in 2017, Nnamdi told Vanity Fair that it was possible he and his wife would co-produce a project at some point.

“We would produce,” he said. “We’d probably produce before acting, I would think.” The following year, his production company, iAm21 Entertainment, helped produce American Son, a Broadway play starring Kerry, and hopefully, that won’t be the last time the two are involved in the same project.

As far as sharing the screen goes, however, Nnamdi doesn’t think that will ever happen. As he told The New York Times, “I produced American Son, but as actors, there’s no plan as of now for that collaboration. We’re very supportive of each other’s journeys, but we’ve always been that way. We always want the best for each other in whatever we’re doing.”

Case in point? The couple’s *chef’s kiss* appearance at the 2025 Met Gala. Ahem:

Oh, and the duo’s 2025 Golden Globes looks:

And ofc, he was right by Kerry's side during her December 2024 Hollywood Walk of Fame ceremony, where she finally received her well-deserved star. How cute do they look with Kerry's parents??

TL;DR: Kerry and Nnamdi truly couldn’t be any cuter. Keep on being adorably low-key, you two.

#7061. The Latest PlayStation Is 6 Years Old and Twice as Expensive as When It Debuted, What’s That About?

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Thanks to AI and the RAMpocalypse, it’ll cost you to play the new ‘GTA VI’.

Grand Theft Auto VI is here, and it’s going to shock people heading into the holidays. No, we’re not moralizing about whatever violence, sex, or illegal activity is in the game. We’re talking about sticker shock.

Do you have any idea how much a new entry-level PlayStation 5 costs now? It costs $600, yet it was only $400 when it debuted in 2020. And the entry-level Xbox? It’ll also run you $600, though you can get a model with less storage for $500. Also having launched in 2020, the lower-storage Xbox Series S used to retail for $300. Both those consoles are more than half a decade old, and they have increased in price by 50 percent.

So what does a consumer do? I’m imagining a guy—perhaps this is you, reader. The last time he bought a console was a PlayStation 3 in 2013 for the previous Grand Theft Auto, or even a PS4 in 2018 for Red Dead Redemption 2. He thought about getting a PS5 for EA Sports College Football in 2024 but ultimately passed.This guy doesn’t pay attention to video games—or he only pays attention to the odd AAA release—and he is expecting to pay $400 for the key to experiencing this generation’s only game that matters. He might not even know that the game itself is going for $80, a rarity that only Nintendo has tried, with Mario Kart World. When he walks up to the counter, this hypothetical man will be paying nearly $900 to be able to play GTA VI. And the worst part? He’s not hypothetical. Starting Thursday, there are going to be thousands of real people in this very situation. Here’s why and what you can do—can you do anything?—about it.

GTA VI has taken over a decade to make (that’s longer than it took Rome to build the Colosseum), and estimates put the budget somewhere north of $1 billion. Rockstar has pushed back, though, with head of publishing Jennifer Kolbe telling Bloomberg that the rumor of the budget surpassing the $1.5 billion budget of the Burj Khalifa is just that—a rumor. Still, it’s not a stretch to call this the biggest media release of all time.

It’s also the biggest test of gaming’s resiliency to price hikes—price hikes that (like everything else, it seems) tie back to AI.

“When [EA Sports College Football] came back a couple of years ago, there was an influx of people who hadn’t bought a console yet,” said Mat Piscatella, a video game industry analyst and advisor at Circana. “Those folks at the time mostly chose either the Xbox Series S or the PlayStation 5 Digital, so the least expensive options to get in.”

The main cause is that the multibillion-dollar gaming industry is in a crisis, a RAM crisis. The RAMageddon, or RAMpocalypse, is a global supply shortage of memory chips that is primarily caused by the post-2024 boom in generative AI. Cleanview, a market-intelligence company, reported that the total number of data centers rose from 361 in 2016 to 1,278 in 2026, with more than five hundred more being built. Data centers are giant warehouses full of computer systems, complex computer systems requiring unprecedented amounts of RAM.

RAM stands for random-access memory. Memory, in this instance, doesn’t mean storage space. RAM is your computer’s “short-term memory,” essentially how many different things your computer’s brain can process at once. The more RAM your computer has, the more it can handle simultaneously. And according to JP Morgan Global Research, prices have risen more than 400 percent in the past two years.

That gets us back to GTA VI and the world of video game consoles. Modern games, with their massive open worlds, highly detailed graphics, and complex physics simulations, require a ton of RAM. As computer parts become scarcer and more expensive, the price of a console goes up. Thus, consoles that—besides the higher-tier PlayStation 5 Pro—have not had meaningful updates in half a decade are now hundreds of dollars more expensive than they were at launch.

Because contrary to what people know about the old gaming business models, the machines themselves still need to turn a profit. It didn’t use to be this way. In the PS2 era, consoles were sold at a loss, with games being the moneymaker. Things changed, audiences grew and moved elsewhere, and—somewhere between microtransactions and Xbox Game Pass—platform holders needed (or preferred) to make money on hardware again. So if a console is more expensive to manufacture, Sony and Microsoft aren’t going to eat that price. Consumers are seeing that increase directly, perhaps more directly and more acutely than we’ve ever seen any increase in technology prices.

Here’s our advice: Don’t wait. The AI push shows no signs of slowing down, and it’s likely that this RAM pipeline bottleneck won’t fix itself for a year or two. This holiday season is getting underway with a supply and demand squeeze similar to what we saw in 2020, when these consoles originally launched.

If you can swing it, you’ll probably want to buy a new console, but the PlayStation 5 Pro is already impossible to find brand-new. It might come down to shopping for renewed and refurbished consoles. These give you a very slight discount, but really they just give you a console that you can plug in and fire up GTA VI on. Welcome to the future of console gaming: refurbished six-year-old consoles.

#7060. The Property Brothers Reveal Why Lighting Tops Their Holiday Chore Checklist

The holidays are upon us, and while we busy our hands with turkeys, trees, and tinsel, the Property Brothers are gifting fans a few friendly tips for hosting across generations. Partnering with Home Instead, Drew and Jonathan Scott have developed a holiday chore checklist that every host should complete before guests arrive.

“The holidays are all about bringing family together. But when you're hosting aging loved ones, a little preparation can go a long way in making sure everyone feels comfortable and safe. As home renovation experts, we know that it's often the smallest changes that make the biggest difference,” the brothers explain.

Ahead, we’re sharing their top tips for a safe, festive, and inviting home this holiday season.

While string lights and extension cords are all holiday decor staples, they can be a major tripping hazard. To prevent spending the holidays in the emergency room, the brothers recommend double-checking that loose ends and loops are neatly tucked away to keep paths and walkways clear throughout your home.

Slippery rugs, mats, and runners are another major safety hazard. Drew and Jonathan recommend reinforcing any pieces that could potentially slide with gripping tabs or replacing them with options that have better traction—especially in bathrooms where wet floors pose a greater risk.

While decorating with Christmas lights is at the top of everyone’s to-do list, your home needs more than a well-lit tree. Dark halls and stairways are major safety concerns, especially when hosting guests unfamiliar with your floor plan. Plug-in nightlights are a great option for improved visibility, and they can be decorative too.

A clear entryway for the holidays is a nonnegotiable. Outside the home, leaves and debris lining your front steps are as dangerous as they are unsightly. Before your guests ring the doorbell, take the time to rake, sweep, and clean as much as you can to ensure all steps, dips, and curbs are visible.

Drew and Jonathan clock in at well over six feet tall, but their guests may not be as gifted with height. If you’re used to storing things up high, the brothers recommend moving essentials like plates and glasses from high shelves and into lower, more easily accessible territory. Not only does this minimize straining, but it also reduces the risk of dropping pieces that could leave dangerous, messy shards in their wake.

#7051. Designers Reveal the Fastest Ways They've Upgraded a Kitchen Under $100

No renovation needed for these designer-approved swaps.

As you whip up a weeknight meal or prep for a dinner party, you might find yourself daydreaming—and envisioning the kitchen you wish you had. While your current cook space might be perfectly functional, it just doesn’t have the aesthetics you long for.

Alas, the kitchen is widely regarded as the most expensive room to renovate. As Houzz reports, the average cost to remodel a kitchen is currently $26,500. But if you’re happy with things like cabinets and appliances (or a renovation is far off in the future), and you want to focus on just the visuals, you can give the space a glow-up with as little as $100.

It can indeed be done, and ahead, designers are sharing their best budget-friendly upgrades.

Rugs are such thrift-able items. A good rug is basically a necessity in a kitchen, and it can easily be swapped with a new one that refreshes the visuals.

Brooke Mennen-Talsma, principal interior designer at Brooke MT Interiors, shares that in the above kitchen she designed, she added a thrifted runner that she discovered for just $40, adding a focal point to an arched pantry.

She says to not underestimate the power of secondhand, and a lot of her favorite pieces in her portfolio are thrifted items.

Switching in fresh hardware for your cabinets is something that can be done in an afternoon, and it can be gentle on the wallet, too. You can also choose to add backplates to your existing hardware, as Sherrell Neal of Sherrell Design Studio recommends. She calls it a “quick upgrade that can stay under $100,” depending on the size and number of cabinets.

While new lighting in a kitchen can quickly add up, you can add light in other, more economical ways.

For instance, as Kira Young, founder and principal designer at High Tide Design, says, you can simply go with a “cute countertop lamp with a pretty little shade.” She advises specifically purchasing a rechargeable lamp, with no messy cords and no need for an outlet. It can also be easily moved anywhere.

Homeowners are increasingly opting for collected, and even pleasantly cluttered, looks in their homes. But if you really want your kitchen visuals to look polished and pleasing, Heather Kirk, principal interior designer at Kirk Riley Design, suggests highlighting a “hero piece,” or a favorite item that shines and steals focus.

Think strong statement pieces that offer both beauty and function, “like a cheerful tea kettle on the stove or a dramatic footed bowl on the counter,” as Kirk recommends.

Emily Spanos, creative director of Emily June Designs, says that in one kitchen she designed, she painted only the lower cabinets a bright turquoise and kept the walls and upper cabinets a soft white. Paint is one of the cheapest ways to quickly upgrade a home, and it’s something that can effectively be used in the kitchen.

“Painting one element costs a fraction of painting everything, but still highlights what you want the eye to fall on,” Spanos says. “A gallon of paint and a weekend’s work keeps you under budget.”

In addition to going with lower cabinets, you could consider painting an island, pantry door, the trim, or the inside of a glass-front cabinet.

To allow your eye to have multiple things to admire in your kitchen, go with a “layered look,” as designers call it. Think: linens, decor, rugs, and artwork.

Spanos says that she has accomplished this look for under $100 in a few projects she’s worked on. In one art-filled home, she carried the creative feel into the kitchen by displaying art on a couple shelves above the sink area. In another kitchen, she laid down a black-and-white geometric rug for visual interest, which tied into the matte black hardware.

She says, “A few quality tea towels, a small rug or runner, and a piece of art can all be found affordably, especially if you shop vintage or, even better, your own home.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#7049. Meet the Creators Joining the World of Cosmopolitan

Hearst’s newest initiative, Hearst Creator Studios, is bringing a new generation of voices to Cosmo.

Big news: Cosmopolitan’s creator community is growing. We are joining Hearst Creator Studios (HCS), a new platform for handpicked creators to co-produce and distribute original, editorial, and branded content across Hearst brands, including Delish, ELLE, Good Housekeeping, MotorTrend, and, of course, Cosmopolitan. Think of it as a mix of talent agency and creative studio, bringing together creator talent and editorial authority.

In light of the news, we called up nine of our favorite friends and creators to help kick this partnership off. Some are already very familiar to Cosmo readers, including beloved columnists Ella Snyder and Julia Mervis. Others are joining the Cosmo world for the first time, and each brings a strong connection with their audience and a strong POV on the things Cosmo readers really care about.

Get to know them below, and get excited because all nine creators bring something genuinely fresh to both your feed and ours.

#7043. How a Connected Home Supports a Good Night’s Rest

How a wellness content creator uses Hubspace from The Home Depot to curate an environment for better sleep.

As a certified holistic health coach and wellness content creator, Carolina Salazar knows the importance of a peaceful wind-down routine before bed. She trusts Hubspace from The Home Depot that powers many of the smart devices in her home, to help ease her into a good night’s sleep.

Hubspace, The Home Depot’s exclusive smart home platform, powers more than 250 products across select private brands—like Hampton Bay, Husky, and Vissani—as well as national brand partners. It effortlessly connects everyday essentials, including smart security, indoor and outdoor lighting, appliances, switches, watering controls, and more.

Some of the Hubspace app’s most practical features help users personalize lighting and improve home security. Hubspace Connect, for example, lets you control compatible lighting from a single switch, while Vacation Mode is a security feature that turns lights and smart plugs on and off while you’re away. One of Salazar’s favorite features is Circadian Rhythm, which automatically transitions lights from cooler, whiter tones to warmer ones as the day goes on.

“I can set my lights to follow my circadian rhythm, so they shift automatically throughout the day,” says Salazar. “And when they start to turn warm in the evening, that’s my cue to slow it down.” That can mean cozying up with a book, brewing a cup of hot tea, or, in her case, lighting a candle at her bedside and journaling. When Salazar is finished putting her thoughts to paper, she uses the Hubspace app to adjust the color, temperature, and brightness of her lights, dimming them as she gets closer to sleep. This can even be programmed automatically in order to reduce screen time before bed.

“Colder rooms bring better sleep, so I also have Hubspace turn on my AC at night,” she says.

The Hubspace app is free to download and easily syncs with your home’s smart devices. You can enable voice control for screen-free commands—also a plus if you’re trying to reduce your pre-sleep screen time. And the app runs on both WiFi and Bluetooth—so even if the WiFi is down, your nighttime routine can still run seamlessly.

#7040. Lululemon’s Black Friday Sale Started Early, and It’s Stacked

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Time to start checking off that gift list.

Sure, you can often score pieces from Lululemon’s “We Made Too Much” section, but that’s mostly limited to “get it before it’s gone” colors and styles.

#7038. Take Note: This Is How Cool People Are Decorating for Thanksgiving This Year

These tastemakers are bringing their personal style to the holiday.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pumpkin centerpieces, rustic garlands, dried-fruit wreaths, and cornucopias are classic Thanksgiving decorations. They’re timeless and beloved, but they can also feel predictable. Traditional decor has its place, of course, but Thanksgiving tablescapes—and dinner parties in general—are evolving, much like interiors, to feel more personal, lived-in, and layered. So we asked nine tastemakers and experts how they’re decorating for this family-centric holiday.

From traditional touches to wholly unexpected looks, read on to see how they’re making their homes feel festive this autumn, and maybe you’ll be inspired to try something new this year.

Ever since designer Mikel Welch moved to sunny California, his family has never hesitated to go to his for Thanksgiving to escape the chilly Michigan weather. It’s become a tradition to gather at his house, and similarly to how he grew up, Welch likes to keep his decorations for the holiday understated and warm.

“I love getting the exterior of my house into full fall mode with mums, pumpkins, and seasonal greenery,” Welch tells House Beautiful. “The beauty of fall decorating is that it can carry you from October straight through Thanksgiving without having to change much.”

The designer’s house is filled with dark, moody colors and earthy textures year-round, so he focuses mainly on bringing natural elements inside to do the work for him. “I also have a little tradition with my dad,” he adds. “For the past couple of years, we've made trips to the nursery together to pick out flowers, greenery, and sometimes bales of hay for the front of the house. It's become something I really enjoy doing with him.”

“Thanksgiving has always been a big family affair,” real estate expert and maximalism extraordinaire Harvin Greene says. She’s happy to keep that tradition going as an adult, just in her own way. In the past, Greene’s aunt and uncle would have everyone at their house for the big meal, but last year, they passed the hosting baton to Greene for the first time. “I may have gotten a little carried away...in the best possible way.”

Getting to decorate two tables—one for the “adults,” another for the “kids,” even though they’re technically all grown-ups, too—Greene used this opportunity to design two completely different tablescapes. “I grew up surrounded by women who understood that a beautifully set table and a genuinely good time should go hand in hand,” she adds.

The one in the formal dining space (i.e., the “adults’” table) was theatrical and dramatic, and nearly everything on it had some sort of familial connection, from her parents’ engraved goblets to her husband’s grandmother’s sterling flatware. The kids’ table, on the other hand, was whimsical and vibrant, complete with a yellow patterned tablecloth, colorful glassware, and playful animal candlesticks. “I don't feel the need to follow the traditional Thanksgiving color palette,” Greene says. “Who decided Thanksgiving had to be orange and brown? Yellow, pink, turquoise, and green can be just as festive.”

The holiday isn’t over until they bring out the Bingo cards, a spirited Thanksgiving tradition that Greene’s Aunt Jean started—which involves cash prizes. “It's one of those family traditions that makes the holiday feel like ours, no matter whose house we're celebrating in.”

Talia Hubble is no stranger to a good dinner party—much of her content actually centers around her love of hosting. But Thanksgiving is a bit extra special for the Rhode Islander, especially since it’s being held at her cottage this year. “Growing up, it was just me and my mom, and she was too busy to decorate,” Hubble says. “I think that’s part of why I’ve fallen in love with seasonal decorating as an adult, I get to bring all my cozy childhood decorating dreams to life!”

As soon as the calendar switches from October to November, Hubble starts decorating for fall, opting for an eclectic mix of subtle Christmas decor (think bows, garlands, and wreaths), an excessive amount of taper candles, and a traditional Thanksgiving palette of oranges, browns, greens, and reds. Every year looks a little bit different, but there are pieces in her collection that are simply too cute not to bring out every holiday season.

“Half the fun of pulling everything out for Thanksgiving is remembering where it came from,” she says. “I think the best decor is a culmination of pieces you’ve been collecting for years, and the memories behind those pieces are what make the table feel special and uniquely yours.”

By the time she’s done decorating, her home feels like a warm, welcoming escape, somewhere her guests can exhale. “My philosophy is that you can’t go wrong when you aim for cozy, colorful, and embrace a little organized mess,” Hubble adds. “My favorite Thanksgiving tables are the ones that look even better halfway through dinner, when the napkins are rumpled and everyone’s reaching for seconds.”

Chef and food stylist Romilly Newman can’t always count on being in the same place as her family for Thanksgiving since everyone’s scattered about, but she always makes a point to throw an autumnal dinner with friends around the holiday time, complete with beautiful fall decor.

Her mom never decorated their home while she was growing up with specific Thanksgiving decorations—instead, she would decorate with deep orange and red flowers to capture the fall vibe. Newman’s style as an adult is similar. “I am fall-obsessed, so as soon as the temperature drops from summer, I'm setting up branches, candles, and darker accents,” she says. “I love the atmosphere inside and outside around Thanksgiving time, and I try my best to bottle that up in my home.”

Natural thicker linen, pops of silver, dark florals, and moody lighting envelop her space as the chef relies mostly on candlelight and organic elements—like figs, sage, chestnuts, and mandarins—as the decorative accents.

“Thanksgiving is great because it brings family together and offers time to enjoy food, friends, and being at home,” Newman adds. “Whatever your style is, try to create an environment that allows for great conversation and a festive evening. You want everyone to be comfortable and feel the holiday spirit.”

When writer Olivia Noceda was a child, she would stay up late the night before Thanksgiving and design custom menus for everyone on Microsoft Paint, which she would then print out, frame, and place on each table setting in preparation for the big meal. The holiday was a big deal for Noceda’s mother, who would always put on the most beautiful Thanksgiving gatherings. “I think that's where my love for hosting and setting a beautiful table really started,” she explains.

However, for the last six years, Noceda has been having Thanksgiving with her husband’s side of the family, so when she does host around the holiday, it’s typically for Friendsgiving. “I don't necessarily pick a theme, but I do like everything to feel cohesive,” she explains of her decorating process. “I usually start incorporating little autumnal touches sometime in October and let them carry me through Thanksgiving.”

The writer and recipe developer lets the season guide her, building her tablescape and decor around a beautiful branch from the farmers’ market, a bowl of pears or persimmons, a collection of little pumpkins and squash, or a few taper candles on the dining room table. Ingredients play a big part in her decor—she can even take a fresh loaf of bread and make it feel special on her table.

For the rest of her decor, she likes to incorporate vintage serving pieces, everyday elements, and elevated details like antique silver and linen napkins with things she already owns to lean into that lived-in, warm feeling. The last thing she wants is her home to feel like a department store display. “Nothing needs to match perfectly, and I actually prefer when it doesn't,” Noceda adds.

What entrepreneur and Real Housewives of New York star Erin Lichy remembers best about Thanksgivings growing up is the smell. “We always spent Thanksgiving out east, and the house was full of pumpkins, local hay, and lanterns,” she explains. The scent of that fresh produce, mixed with her grandmother’s homemade turkey t’bit, has shaped the way she decorates for and hosts Thanksgiving. “That coveted and highly personal (and incredibly delicious) recipe is in my cookbook, She’s a Host, and it’s part of every Thanksgiving I host.”

Decorating starts in the kitchen for her family. Lichy wants the home to smell like fall, so she focuses on that sense, making seasonal recipes with squash and pumpkin and incorporating autumnal flowers in her interiors. The melding of those scents gives her townhouse a warm feeling, especially when mixed with layered fall colors and the crackling of the fireplace.

Ever since Lichy and her family have moved into their townhouse, she’s been obsessed with decorating her stoop too. “The stoop is the first thing everyone sees, so this year I decided to bring in Simon Wallace Design to make it really stand out!” Lichy adds. “They completely transformed it with layers of seasonal color and texture, and it feels so warm and welcoming.” She specifically loves using the faux garlands and pinecones, as well as lanterns, from Balsam Hill and Frontgate because they’re so beautifully made that they look real, and she’s able to reuse them year after year.

No matter what she does to decorate though, she just wants her home to feel warm and lived-in, and this is the season and holiday to really lean into that.

Decorating for the holidays is Gabby Amato’s version of a self-care day. “It's truly one of my favorite days of the year,” she says. “I carve out a weekend afternoon when my husband and son are out of the house, light my favorite fall candles, turn on some music, and have the house entirely to myself.”

Amato, the founder of Table Two Studio, a paper plate brand which specializes in beautiful, artistic designs that you definitely won’t want to throw away, credits her love of Thanksgiving and the fall season in general to her mother. While growing up in the ‘90s, she saw her mom put so much care and effort into the holiday decorations, always making their home feel warm, nostalgic, and full of spirit. “For Thanksgiving, she decorated our mantel with gourds, greenery, and ceramic pilgrim statues that were a gift from my grandmother,” Amato says. “We had oversized cornstalks and pumpkins at the front door. Nothing ever felt overly styled or precious.”

It’s this feeling that has stuck with her throughout her life, and it’s what she hopes she captures in her own home every year. She begins decorating for the fall early, right after Labor Day, pulling out old pieces and unboxing anything new and arranging it all in fresh ways so nothing feels stale. “I love beautiful decor, of course, but I'm much more interested in creating an atmosphere than achieving a particular aesthetic,” she adds. “I want my home to feel warm, lived-in, a little nostalgic, and festive.”

Donna Lennard’s family was never one for holiday dressing—most of the attention at Thanksgiving fell on the delicious food shared with family. As a restauranteur and cofounder of beloved NYC establishment il Buco, it makes sense why the meal remains to be the most important thing to Lennard even today.

“Clearly, the table is always festive, whether it’s the tablecloth, the napkins, or linens that reflect the season in burnt orange and the like,” she explains. “I love to decorate a table with assorted colorful gourds and seasonal florals, beautiful candlelight (our beeswax candles already have an orange glow), orange ranunculus, and the occasional dried floral.” Colorful Pienza glassware dots the table and her plethora of beautiful tableware gives it more character, but she keeps everything else relatively simple to ensure the food remains the center of attention.

Though Lennard doesn’t often host her family and friends for the Thanksgiving meal, she’s already looking forward to next year when everyone will finally gather at her beach house.

Being from Australia, Kendall Knox—the cofounder of Olive Ateliers along with her husband—didn’t have a personal reference of what the holiday “should” be or look like. This gives her the freedom of deciding what it is for her family. “What it has become is an intimate table: my husband Ben, our son Rhodes, and Ben's mum,” she says. “No rotation, no kids' table, no forty-pound bird. Just the four of us and a dinner that runs long because nobody is in a hurry to leave it.”

Since Knox isn’t beholden to the classic Thanksgiving decor, she tends to keep her home and her table rather simple, “shopping the house,” as she puts it to decorate instead of buying something new. She keeps a rather neutral palette of rich browns, creamy whites, terracotta, and silver, then uses antique linens, mismatched silverware, and candlesticks that have collected wax to capture the true lived-in feel.

Her focus tends to be on the table and the meal, letting the food add more color. If a tablecloth has a wine ring on it from the year before, all the better—it complements the stacked plates at the end of the meal.

“The objects that come out every year, that get a little more marked each time, are the ones that end up meaning the most,” Knox adds. “Thanksgiving is just a very good excuse to use them.”

On top of being the magazine's go-to Waco expert, Meghan also touches on quite a few other verticals in the digital space, like hot news content featuring your favorite HGTV stars, home design inspiration, and the latest interior TikTok trends. When she's not interviewing interior designers about home trends, you can find Meghan reading a fictional thriller with her cat in her lap, planning themed parties, or strolling through flea markets searching for chunky candle holders and vintage bookends.

#7037. The Only Apple Deals Worth Your Dollar This Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

AirPods, MacBooks, and Watches for up to 28% off—this year’s sale is hot.

If it feels like life is one long string of sales—most of them being mediocre—you’re not wrong. Despite the near-constant deluge of Prime Days, anniversary events, and the like, some sales do legitimately stand above the rest. You know the one: Black Friday. It tends to bring some of the best deals of the year, including record-low prices on Apple tech.

Apple is notorious for not marking down its own products—on the contrary, the tech giant actually raised prices on Macs and iPads earlier this year—so we turn to retailers like Amazon when looking to save on everything from AirPods to Apple Watches. This year, Amazon’s Black Friday deals span 12 days (the sale is called Turkey 12 if you’d like to know too much), which means plenty of Apple discounts are already live. In the event that you’ve spilled coffee on your MacBook or lost one of your AirPods, you needn’t wait until Black Friday proper to shop.

Sure, there’s always the chance these deals could slightly deepen, but we’d wager we’re only looking at marginal price cuts at this point. Consider this: Shop now, shave a couple hundred bucks off of those tech upgrades you’ve been avoiding, then spend your holiday weekend watching football and reheating Thanksgiving leftovers instead of scrolling through Amazon. Your move.

AirPods reliably go on deep discount during Amazon sale events, and Black Friday has proven no different. The third-generation AirPods Pro have dropped to just $179, only $10 shy of their record-low price.

Although the new AirPods 5 aren’t marked down right now, they start at just $129, which is still lower than the discounted Pros. We’re hoping that these will go on sale for the first time on Black Friday. For those who prefer over-ear headphones, the AirPods Max 2 are $120 off (not quite their lowest price, but nothing to scoff at). You can also save on Beats wireless headphones and earbuds, including $150 off the ultra-popular Beats Studio Pro, which have Apple’s chip under the hood.

No Apple product experienced higher price hikes than laptops. The MacBook Air jumped $200 to $1,299 and the MacBook Pro climbed $400 to $1,999, driven by rising RAM and storage costs. To ease the pain, both the 13- and 15-inch MacBook Air models are up to 19% off. We haven’t seen deals this deep on Macs in quite some time.

If you prefer the Pro, those aren’t quite as discounted, but you can still shave up to $250 off of a souped-up model. Plus, Apple’s most affordable laptop, the MacBook Neo, just went on sale for the first time. Sure, it’s only a $50 discount, but it’s still the cheapest model by far—and fits the bill for most basic needs like watching Netflix and renewing your lease.

Although Apple Watches haven’t fallen victim to price increases this year, deals have been few and far between, which means these Black Friday discounts are liable to sell out fast.

The brand-new Apple Watch Series 12—which ushered in Apple’s first stab at readiness scores—is $70 off, dropping this to its lowest price ever. If you’re looking for a budget-friendly Apple Watch, opt for the SE 3, which has dipped to under $200—a verifiable steal given Apple’s current pricing dynamics. While the rugged Apple Watch Ultra 4 isn’t on sale right now, we’ll keep you posted should any surprise sales arise.

Apple Watch Series 12 (GPS+ Cellular, 42mm)

Back in June, Apple bumped the price of its entire iPad range by up to $200, which makes scoring one on sale all the more crucial. As predicted, previous-gen iPad Minis (an editor favorite) are $100 off after the revamped A20 Pro model was released earlier this month. Should you prefer a full-sized tablet, entry-level iPads, iPad Airs, and iPad Pros are all on sale, so it’s mostly a matter of use case in determining which to buy. If you’re going to watch TV on it while traveling, opt for the $399 model, while the Pro is better suited for getting creative work done.

Accessories are reliably among the easiest Apple products to save on during major sales. If you want to stick with Apple-branded gear, the second-generation AirTag, complete with improved Precision Finding, is sitting at a record-low $23, in addition to discounts on Apple Pencils, Magic Keyboards, and the like. You can also save on plenty of third-party power banks, wall adapters, and wireless chargers, which tend to see steeper discounts than Apple’s own gear.

#7036. 7 Holiday Decor Trends Designers Say We're About to See Everywhere

The pros are leaning into handmade holiday style.

Pictured above: A holiday-ready space by designer Marie Flanigan.

Christmas is just around the corner, and as the holiday spirit makes its way into our homes, it’s a good time to give your seasonal decor a refresh. This year, on-trend doesn’t have to mean brand-new—designers love decorating with heirloom pieces collected over time.

However, if you’re in the mood to try something a little different, here are seven trends on our radar for a chic Christmas.

This winter, our decor is taking a note from European culture—again. Swedish folk art is emerging as a hot winter trend in both holiday and home decor. Defined by bold red hues, tulip motifs, and S-scrolls, the style blends cozy-core with the heritage style craze.

“European folk influences are so big right now,” says designer Johanna Lyle of Jo Lyle & Co. “Think Swedish Dala horses, quilt motifs, and decorative stitching. I think we will see those continue into holiday decor.”

When it comes to her tree decor, interior designer Francesca Grace waves a hand at the carefully contrived, and she’s not alone. “My ornaments are collected from my travels, so the tree is really a collection of memories. I love unpacking them and remembering where each one came from. They don’t all match, and I wouldn’t want them to,” says Grace. “I let the ornaments lead. That mix feels much more personal to me, and it gives the tree something new to say as the collection grows.” This year’s trends hinge on antiqued, inherited style, with one-off pieces that channel bold character and hardly ever come with a matching set.

Interior designer Marie Flanigan likes to add a fresh twist to her seasonal decor with dried oranges and pops of cranberry red.

“We've seen such a beautiful resurgence of dried citrus in holiday decorating over the past few years,” Flanigan explains. “And I think cranberries are ready for their moment to shine. Their rich red hue feels especially festive, and I love the idea of incorporating them into fresh greenery, draping them across a mantel, or pairing them with dried oranges for a more layered look.”

If there’s one classic Christmas element Grace hopes makes a comeback, it's multicolored lights. Inherently nostalgic, these bold-hued bulbs were a ’90s staple.

“I’m ready for a little less restraint on the tree. Those reds, blues, greens, and yellows immediately bring back the feeling of Christmas as a child. They also pull together a collection of ornaments that might otherwise seem completely unrelated. White lights can be beautiful, but I want my Christmas tree to have some mischief,” says Grace.

The classic red-and-green combination may take the title for the most iconic holiday palette, but this year, color trends are going complementary. While base colors stay warm and slightly rusted, patterned pieces are taking on cooler tones. This year, we’re also trading traditional plaid for Scottish tartan.

“Warm crimsons, rich browns, tactile fabrics, and blue-green plaids make a beautiful holiday palette,” says Elizabeth Bennett, cofounder of the interior design firm Kobel + Co.

In a total rejection of mass-manufactured everything, handmade felt pieces are taking holiday homes by storm. The style has even coined a new holiday aesthetic: #Kitschmas, and, according to Lyle, it’s here to stay. “As AI continues to homogenize design, things made by humans will feel more special and important. Handmade and crafted items create stories around decor that feel meaningful and can be treasured year after year,” she says.

Moody browns are steadily emerging as the new neutral as we inch closer to the new year, and lucky for our winter design schemes, the hue really is as versatile as it seems. Paired with powdery blues, bold crimsons, and even minty greens, chocolate brown grounds nearly any palette and matches every metallic hue.

“I’m particularly drawn to chocolate brown as an unexpected foundation for holiday color palettes. It's incredibly rich and versatile. I love the idea of moving beyond traditional red and green and allowing the existing colors of a room to inform the holiday decor. You can create something incredibly festive through tonal layering, subtle metallic accents, and variations in material,” Flanigan explains.

#7034. Dreaming of Buying a Home Abroad? These 10 Countries Make It Shockingly Simple

If you’ve always wanted to live out your expat fantasies, start here.

Over the last few years, many Americans have daydreamed about life abroad. Moving internationally can often feel like a pipe dream, but with the right planning, it’s possible. However, lengthy visa processes, daunting language barriers, and a difficult-to-navigate legal system can make the dream feel more like a nightmare.

Certain countries, like Australia, Canada, and New Zealand, have bans on foreign buyers. Others, like Thailand, Cuba, and Vietnam, don’t allow outsiders to own property at all. On the other end of the spectrum are countries actively trying to attract foreign buyers, offering enviable incentives like affordable home prices, a smoother customs process, and an easier-to-navigate work-visa approval process.

Choosing the right location can make moving abroad much less intimidating. Ahead, we’re breaking down the ten best countries for Americans looking to buy property abroad. Whether you’re dreaming of relocating or searching for your perfect vacation home, these countries make buying as easy as possible and offer gorgeous landscapes too.

If you’ve ever dreamed of living among medieval architecture and rolling mountain ranges, Georgia might be the key to unlocking your fantasy. A bridge between Eastern Europe and Western Asia, the transcontinental country borders Russia, Turkey, Armenia, and the Black Sea, offering both subtropical and high-altitude climates. Thankfully, the real estate landscape is much less complex, with few legal or bureaucratic hurdles for American buyers, no residency or visa required (for up to a year), and remarkably fast processing times. In fact, the only major restriction in the Georgia market is that foreigners cannot purchase or own agricultural land, a nod to the importance of the local, small-scale farming cultures peppered across the country’s regions.

Uruguay’s buying process could not be more straightforward. South America’s second-smallest sovereign country requires no citizenship, visa, or prior approvals. Under Uruguayan law, foreign buyers are given the same rights as citizens and enjoy the benefits of free capital flow. Locals are just as, if not more, tourist-friendly. The country is known for its hospitality, laid-back café culture, and stunning coastal landscapes.

Instead of retiring to Florida, consider this balmy, beautiful Caribbean country—just a four-hour direct flight from the New York area. Like everything else on the island, the real estate laws are relatively relaxed, making buying a house easy-breezy; and you can buy and own property without obtaining residency status or special permissions, as is the case in many other Caribbean islands. “Foreigners can own property outright in the Dominican Republic, with legal protections for foreign investors,” says Mauricio Umansky, CEO and founder of The Agency. One important consideration is financing: You can certainly get a mortgage in the DR, but it may not be quite as cushy as what you can get in the U.S.—so pay cash if possible.

Great news if you’ve been wanting a more provincial life: Americans face zero restrictions when buying homes in France. The open market allows for statesiders to buy houses, apartments, or plots of land in the city, countryside, and coastal towns in person or remotely without needing citizenship, residency, or even a local visa. However, a purchase does not grant residency, so if you’re hoping to make France your permanent home, you’ll want to apply for a separate long-stay visa and note the Schengen 90-out-of-180-day rule. While buying may be simple, the finances are anything but. Closing fees, including notary fees, typically add seven to ten percent to your purchase. Mortgage rates are also incredibly hard to secure for U.S.-based buyers, thanks to strict debt-to-income caps and FATCA compliance. To simplify the process, many buyers opt to put 20 percent to 40 percent down in cash.

Perhaps the most American-friendly market, Panama boasts a large English-speaking market, virtually no restrictions on foreign property ownership, and uses the U.S. dollar as legal tender in a vastly affordable market. The Central American nation also offers residency programs like the Friendly Nations Visa, clearing the way legally for residency by investment. In addition to an efficient legal system and reliable market, Panama boasts breathtaking beaches, lush rainforests, and exceptional wildlife.

If you’re hoping for a home in the coffee valleys of Colombia, you’ll need to hire a real estate lawyer before you start scouting for mountain chalets, coffee triangle treehouses, and rainforest retreats. The Caribbean country opts out of standardized agent licensing systems, making legal aid essential to verify title and freedom certificates. While citizenship isn’t required, you will need to secure a tourist visa and Colombian Tax ID to complete your purchase and register for property taxes.

The buying process in Greece is lengthy, though it is streamlined, with a clear checklist for American buyers to find success in the market. Hiring a local lawyer or notary is a great first step after scouting properties. Not only are they familiar with title checks and contracts, but they can also navigate the intricacies that come with buying in designated border areas or near military zones, which require special clearance for non–EU buyers. While the most popular areas for American buyers, like Athens and Crete, don’t come with heavy legal intricacies, buying any property in Greece will mean securing a Greek Tax Number—a small price to pay for cities with such rich histories and breathtaking seaside views.

The people in this popular Western European coastal country are famously friendly and inviting, and that extends to real estate. According to Samira Easton, a certified international property specialist with Premier Sotheby’s International Realty, Portugal offers a Golden Visa Program, which makes it simple to buy property and gain residency; clear real estate laws; and a well-established network of English-speaking real estate agents, lawyers, and notaries to help you during the process.

Belize is home to one of the most vibrant, tourist-friendly cultures in Central America, mixing Mayan, Hispanic, Asian, and Creole cultures in its dense rainforests and rolling low plains. Buying here is as easy as it can get, as outright ownership applies to foreigners, and a British-inspired legal system abolishes language barriers and makes contracts easier to understand. Even more, there is no capital gains tax in Belize, and property taxes trend low. The country also boasts vibrant marine life and is home to the world’s second-largest barrier reef.

Spain may have ended its investor visa program in 2025, but the real estate market is still remarkably easy for Americans to navigate. The country currently imposes no restrictions on foreign or nonresident property ownership, but there are a few things you’ll need to procure before you buy, like a Foreigner Identification Number, which is mandatory for taxes. Despite the minor hurdles, the market is booming both on the ground and online. “There are also numerous online resources and property portals available, so it’s easy to find listings and information,” Easton says. This is a boon for those who aren’t able to make the trip over to Europe to house shop in person.Note that buying property does not grant you residency or bypass the country’s 90-day Schengen limit. If you’re hoping to turn your Spanish holiday into your everyday, you’ll have to apply for a separate visa.

Minimalist, maximalist, or somewhere in between? Find your holiday makeup match at Ulta Beauty.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Are you a five-minute-face person, or does your beauty routine involve three eyeshadow brushes, a playlist, and absolutely no rushing? Ahead, discover the best holiday makeup palettes and gift sets at Ulta Beauty, whether you're treating yourself or shopping for someone on your nice list.

#7030. Has Zegna Cracked the Code to Modern Tailoring?

With its latest jacket, the Falcon, the brand brings some sporty utility to its collection of mix-and-matchable clothing. Artistic director Alessandro Sartori explains how it’s all part of a bigger project to help men dress their best.

Pay attention to the world of menswear long enough, and you’ll notice that certain quandaries come up time and again. A favorite among traditionalists and avant-garde types alike is the fate of the suit. It’s been incorrectly declared dead enough times that the handwringing over its demise has largely shifted to questions of its evolving role in the modern guy’s wardrobe: Who wears a suit, and when, and why?

According to Alessandro Sartori, the artistic director of Zegna for a little more than a decade now, there’s another question we should all be asking ourselves: What exactly is a suit these days?

“Everything we do here is grounded in tailoring techniques and in tailoring fabrics,” Sartori tells me over Zoom from his home in Milan. “But I'm a big believer that a suit doesn't necessarily have to be a combination of the classic top and the classic bottom in the same fabric and color.” It can be comprised of those things, he stresses. But the “modern suit,” as he calls it, can also be an intentional pairing of a different kind of top—a chore coat, an overshirt—with a trouser with a different fabrication. The materials and colors should feel complementary and cohesive but needn’t be perfectly matched.

“This is not only a way to give a new life to suiting,” he explains, “but to dress men differently—and, at the same time, keep the quality, the craft, and the beautiful fit that a tailored piece has.”

The idea is to adapt classic techniques to the realities of getting dressed today. “Many fashion brands offer a nice quality, but is not the handmade quality that we do,” Sartori says. “And there are a lot of brands that are offering beautiful, beautiful quality, but they are quite classic.” Since he started in 2016, Sartori’s overarching project has been to populate the gulf between those two modes of production, making clothes that feel contemporary but are rooted in the top-tier fabrics and handwork that helped Zegna become the powerhouse of Italian fashion that it is today.

The latest addition to Sartori’s lineup is a new jacket, the Falcon. It pays homage to the sort of utilitarian jackets Zegna founder Ermenegildo Zegna might have worn while walking the grounds of what is now the Oasi Zegna nature reserve in northern Italy, at the base of the Biellese Alps. (According to company lore, Ermenegildo imagined those walks as being accompanied by the flight of a falcon.)

Sartori acknowledges that the use case, in 2026, might be a little different. “I don't think that people are necessarily using the jacket walking up in the mountains,” he says. The Falcon is more about embracing a “sporty-chic attitude,” whether you’re stomping around the country or the city, and working within the modular framework that the designer has created at Zegna. You can mix and match it with other pieces—both old and new—from the brand, and you can wear it as part of a “modern suit” or on its own.

The new design is cut from Zegna’s Oasi Cashmere for lightweight warmth, and Sartori calls it “a great addition to our vocabulary.” Buttoned patch pockets give you somewhere to stash your stuff, a bowling collar can be flipped up to shield against stiff winds, and an internal drawstring can be cinched at the midsection for a little extra protection against the cold. Ultimately, it’s a familiar-looking piece. But it’s exceptional in its construction and materials and its design—a streamlined take on the hunting jackets of yore—is as considered as how it’s made.

“Long-lasting life is a key factor for what we're doing, and I don't mean only quality-wise,” Sartori says. “I also mean design-wise.”

The designer, for his part, hopes that the Falcon—alongside other modern classics from the brand—will be the sort of thing that guys wear and reinterpret for years before passing it on to the next generation. And as the brand continues to grow and evolve alongside its customer base, one of his biggest aims is to ensure that each item he introduces remains relevant throughout its (ideally long) lifespan.

“It’s like writing a book, and every season I add a chapter,” he says. “Because the story is going on, is moving with our clients, our community of people, our brand, our family. So many layers are … I don’t want to say ready, but cooking. I see a bright future for the brand, following this principle of walking with our customers and building an indelible aesthetic. I like this approach.”

#7029. Mckenna Grace Is Winning the Method Dressing Hunger Games

We spoke to her stylist, Molly Dickson, about creating Mckenna's killer carpet looks for the Sunrise on the Reaping press tour.

Method dressing for press tours is part of the reason 2026’s year of movies feels so unstoppable. The carpets for the latest Hunger Games film, Sunrise on the Reaping, have been heating up look after look, but no one is catching fire quite like Mckenna Grace. Along with her stylist, Molly Dickson, the pair have been turning out absolute masterpieces, with extraordinary commitment to Hunger Games literary lore: As small as ladybugs in Mckenna’s hair (a nod to the ‘Ladybug, Ladybug’ song Haymitch Abernathy sings to Maysilee Donner in Sunrise on the Reaping) to step out in NYC, and as large as a recreation of Katniss’s truly iconic wedding dress from The Hunger Games 2: Catching Fire.

“Mckenna was absolutely certain from the beginning that she wanted to embrace method dressing for this press tour, and I was completely on board,” Molly tells Cosmopolitan.

The concept of method dressing isn’t new, but it’s been incredibly impactful on movie press runs in particular over the past few years. In practice, actors wear looks that reference niche details of the films they’re promoting. Think Zendaya’s Greek-goddess-forward looks for The Odyssey, Margot Robbie’s Barbie ensembles pulled straight from the dolls themselves, Ariana Grande and Cynthia Erivo in a lifetime’s worth of pink and green for Wicked.

“I think the best method dressing moments are the ones that tell a story, while still feeling like incredible fashion looks of their own,” says Molly. “We wanted the fans to have those moments of recognition and excitement, but we also wanted someone who knew nothing about The Hunger Games to look at Mckenna and simply think she looked incredible!”

So how did they do it? What references were on their mood board? What dress almost never saw the light of day? And most importantly: What’s the story behind THE legendary arena dress from the UK premiere? Superstar stylist Molly Dickson answers our most pressing press tour related questions.

It was a really collaborative process between the two of us. Mckenna would come to me with ideas, and I would figure out how to translate them into fashion. She was the one who said, ‘Ladybugs are part of the movie, we need to do a ladybug look!’ From there, I started sourcing pieces that either incorporated ladybugs directly or finding more unexpected, interesting ways to reference them.

We were very intentional about making sure every look had a connection to some part of the Hunger Games universe. We wanted the references to feel thoughtful and recognizable to the fans, while still creating beautiful fashion moments that felt authentic to Mckenna and her personal style.

I don't think either of us initially realized just how invested the Hunger Games fans would be in all the little references and Easter eggs! Once we started seeing that reaction, it became more fun to dig deeper into the franchise and pull inspiration from different characters and iconic moments. Mckenna knows this world so well, and I loved being able to bring her ideas to life.

Mckenna really wanted to pay homage to the strong female characters throughout the franchise—specifically Katniss and Joanna Mason—which became the inspiration for so many of our looks. As we started fittings, we found ways to incorporate inspiration from Rue as well.

We wanted a mix of more recognizable references and subtle nods rather than exact recreations of costumes from the movies. We used the original costumes and characters as our starting point, but then put our own fashion interpretation on them. I think that's where my creativity really comes in. You want fans to recognize the inspiration, but you also want the looks to feel fresh, elevated, and true to the person wearing them.

I really can't think of a better way to generate excitement and conversation around a movie through fashion than method dressing. It's such a fun way to bring the story beyond the screen, and give fans something deeper to connect with. What I loved most about this tour was seeing the conversations happening online, not just about the fashion itself, but about the characters, the references, and ultimately the film.

I think my personal favorite was Mckenna’s Paris look inspired by Katniss's wedding gown. It was such a major fashion moment—we actually went back and forth on whether it might be too much! Mckenna’s team really encouraged us to go for it, and they were absolutely right.

The most challenging part was figuring out how to recreate the sculptural silver elements. Michael Ngo made experimental custom silver pieces for us, which we paired with a beautiful vintage Monique Lhuillier wedding gown. I loved that the complete look felt like a true tribute to Katniss's iconic gown, but still had its own fashion identity. It was one of those moments where the creativity, collaboration, and a little bit of risk-taking really paid off!

We really wanted to pay homage to the strong female characters throughout the Hunger Games franchise while still staying true to Mckenna's personal style. We wanted each look to tell a story and have a connection to the films, but never feel like she was wearing a costume.

For the press junkets and more casual appearances, we focused on subtle nods to the movies, whether that was through color, texture, or small details that fans might recognize. For the bigger red carpet moments, we had the opportunity to be a little more dramatic and lean into those bigger, more established references. The fun of this tour was finding that balance between fashion and storytelling!

Honestly, I have to give all the credit to Mckenna’s mom for this one! She actually discovered the dress on Instagram and sent it over to me. The brand is ALmée, and they’re based in Vietnam.

I love that this look came together in such an unexpected way. It wasn't something we had custom-designed or planned months in advance. It was really just Mckenna's mom spotting something incredible and all of us realizing how perfectly it fit into the Hunger Games world.

That's one of my favorite things about styling: Sometimes the most exciting looks come from the most unexpected places, and this was definitely one of those moments!

One of the looks I was most excited about, but also the most stumped on creatively, Katniss's iconic Girl on Fire gown. The challenge was figuring out how to translate that moment into something that felt like a beautiful red carpet gown rather than a costume.

Cong Tri immediately came to mind because of their intricate beadwork and craftsmanship. I knew I wanted the fire gown to incorporate beading because of the way it catches the light, especially under red carpet lighting and camera flashes. I felt that was going to be key to creating the illusion of flames and bringing the dress to life.

It was honestly the perfect collaboration. The Cong Tri team was so willing to push the design creatively. After experimenting with different fabrics, textures, and techniques, the end result was exactly what we had hoped for. The dress felt dramatic and powerful, with that obvious nod to Katniss, but still felt like a really beautiful fashion moment. That's always the goal with these kinds of looks!

Ali Royals loves true stories and hates writing in the third person, even though she’s doing it right now. Ali is a poet, writer, and denier of any Carrie Bradshaw allegations. Follow her on Instagram and Substack for her daily Outfit Prompts, where she tells herself stories in order to get dressed.

#7027. The Surprisingly Wholesome Life of a Professional BDSM Practitioner

I don’t fit the gigolo stereotype of washboard abs and silky hair. Plus, I’m married with a daughter. But when powerful women want to experience their most private fantasies, whether in person or online, they hire me.

Have you heard of BDSM? The acronym refers to the sexual acts of bondage and discipline, dominance and submission, and sadism and masochism. While those acts might sound strange to the uninitiated, they’re not uncommon. A 2020 study published in The Journal of Sex Research found that more than 40 percent of people have BDSM fantasies, while about 20 percent have engaged in BDSM. That’s a pretty big client base for people like the subject of this week’s edition of “The Secret Lives of Men.”

Sir Dax, 53, is a professional BDSM practitioner in central England. His clients, who include OnlyFans models and CEOs, pay him for everything from sexy centaur fantasies to submissive experiences in which they go down on him and cook him dinner. The sexual nature of his work doesn’t affect his private life, however. The happily married father, called a “teddy bear” by his daughter, has a normal, everyday life outside the role-play and sex toys. Here’s how he balances it all.

#7015. This Wood Finish Was Once Seen as “Outdated.” But Designers Now Say It's Coming Back.

You might regret ripping out those kitchen cabinets.

For a period of time in the late 1980s to early 2000s, it seemed virtually impossible to enter a house and not come across honey oak. That bright, orange-toned wood finish covered kitchen cabinets, bathroom vanities, even baseboard trim and furniture around the house.

“You’d see it everywhere—cabinetry, trim, stair railings, and floors. It was the go-to wood tone for builders and homeowners alike,” Cathleen Gruver of Gruver Cooley tells House Beautiful. “But by the early 2000s, it started to fall out of favor as people shifted toward cooler, more neutral interiors.”

However, there’s been a noticeable shift in the way people are wanting their interiors to look, and warmer spaces are definitely back in style. With this evolution, can we expect to see a revival of honey oak, as well? We asked a handful of designers if they think this golden wood finish is back, and how they would style it for modern-day interiors.

Elizabeth Bennett and Mallory Robins, the founders of interior design studio Kobel + Co, believe honey oak is back for two reasons: The first is that warm tones throughout the entirety of the home are on the rise; the second is that honey oak has a lot of nostalgic associations for people who grew up in the '80s and '90s and are now homeowners.

“We're seeing a resurgence of styles from this era not only in homes, but in fashion as well, as we harken back to a pre-digital era,” Bennett explains.

“It’s not necessarily a full-blown comeback, but I’ve definitely noticed a soft return—particularly in cabinetry, millwork details, and furniture," Gruver says. “Clients aren’t always asking for 'honey oak' by name, but they are gravitating toward natural wood tones that offer warmth and character.”

Before you take a sledgehammer to those original honey oak cabinets, reconsider this tone by styling it in more modern ways. Keep reading to see fresh ways that designers are working with honey oak in their new projects.

As homeowners gravitate more toward lived-in, inviting interiors, honey oak is a wonderful way to add warmth. Leave it to the professionals to find stylish ways to make this once-outdated wood tone look cool again.

Plenty of designers will tell you to mix wood tones in your home to prevent it from feeling one-note, and the same is encouraged when honey oak makes a big presence. When designer Adina Hall took on the project above, there was too much honey oak in the space for her team to replace or refinish it all, so they embraced it instead.

“We developed a custom stain for the new wood floors, a whitewashed French oak that created a neutral canvas for the structure without competing with the existing honey tone,” Hall says. “We added custom cabinetry finished in a taupe-tone ash veneer, again a neutral tone, and we leaned into crisp whites and some cool tones like sage green and indigo blue as accents to create a balanced palette.”

Heavy amounts of warm woods can overwhelm the eye; it’s important to build a restrained palette and introduce interest with cooler tones, wood stains, sculptural furniture, and contrasting materials. “The key is to give the honey oak a contemporary context in order to make it feel like an intentional material that delivers a punch of warmth in an otherwise cool and architecturally bold yet restrained palette.”

Whether you’re adding in fresh honey oak or you’re trying to work around what’s already in your home, one of the best ways to ensure it feels modern and not like a ‘90s Tuscan-inspired home is choosing an earthy color palette. Muted greens, warm whites, greiges, taupe, light blues—these are all beautiful hues that won’t add even more orange-ish warmth to your space.

“Instead of making honey oak the focus of the room, I let the beauty of the wood shine through the use of clean lines, black matte touches, natural stones, and modern lighting,” designer Nancy Dow of Sierra Living Concepts says. She’s fond of using this wood, and uses these shades to create a modern and fresh appearance.

Rather than “wood drenching” a home in a single tone (as was often seen in the early aughts), Robins explains that current homeowners are exercising more restraint with their finishes. “Rather than the primary finish for a house on trim, floors, and cabinetry, it's being used in more subtle ways—a cased good piece or a kitchen island alone,” she says.

One of the reasons honey oak fell out of style is because it is a very warm tone, and in the world of “millennial gray,” an orange-tinted kitchen or bathroom isn’t very appealing. However, designer Marli Jones of Rebel House design studio believes this can be fixed simply by creating a contrasting palette.

In the kitchen space above, Jones used only honey oak cabinetry in the scullery to contrast the light, bright main kitchen. “It adds a coziness to the scullery and envelops the space in warm, natural tones,” she says. “That same honey color travels across the countertop stone and into the tile backsplash.” The two rooms are connected by the wall of glass and steel, but the change in materiality from the white cabinets to the honey oak creates a dynamic transition. “Warm wood always adds a grounding layer.”

When adding a honey oak piece into your home, do so intentionally. Opt for a “stunning built-in, a custom island, or as an accent detail that brings texture and warmth,” Gruver says. To emphasize the styling, pair the honey oak piece with contemporary hardware or painted elements to help it feel fresh and curated.

While honey oak can work in any interior aesthetic, there are a few styles it looks best in, according to Gruver: updated farmhouse and cottagecore. Basically, “any space that leans into character, craft, and comfort,” she explains. However, she's also seen the tone used successfully in more modern, organic spaces where there's a large emphasis on natural materials and textures. It all depends on how it's balanced, which holds true for any wood tone. She adds, “When styled right, honey oak can absolutely feel current again.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

The state’s energy office decided not to consult with residents, which is just stellar governing all around.

(Permanent Musical Accompaniment to This Post)

Being our semi-regular weekly survey of what’s going down in the several states where, as we know, the real work of governmentin’ gets done and where the rifleman’s stalking the sick and the lame.

There also was a drive-in that showed films of ... ahem ... a sophisticated nature. Anyway, I used to drive through Sutton on my way to my job in the state forest.
